OF JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS,
ABBOT OF PEAPOLIS,
FORMERLY OF SPONHEIM, TO MAXIMILIAN I, CAESAR:
Six Books of Polygraphy.

To these,
besides the Key and the Observations of Adolf von Glauburg, patrician of Frankfurt,
there has newly been added a booklet by the same author, On the Seven Secundeans, or Intelligences moving the spheres after God.

A work of most hidden knowledge, in which the principles of Steganography also lie concealed, and a method most useful and altogether admirable, both in teaching and in learning, is shown to the ingenious.

STRASBOURG,
at the expense of Lazarus Zetzner, bookseller.
1600.

--- Page 007 ---

ROYAL LIBRARY
OF MUNICH.

THE PRINTER TO THE READER,
Greeting.

When, at the urging of certain persons, candid Reader, I had resolved to publish anew at my own expense the Polygraphy of Johannes Trithemius, I diligently applied myself to this: that it might come forth both more correct and enlarged by a new addition. And so, having sought out various editions from everywhere, I diligently compared them with the most recent; and besides, I added also a manuscript exemplar of Jacob Trithemius, brother of Johannes, and, after making a collation with the remaining editions, I found it nearly similar to them, apart from a few readings. Secondly, I appended the same author’s booklet on the Secundeans, or Intelligences moving the sphere after God. It is yours, kind Reader, to take in good part this endeavor of ours, such as it is. Farewell.

TO

--- Page 008 ---

TO THE DIVINE
MAXIMILIAN I,
CAESAR, THE LETTER
OF JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS,
Abbot of St. James at Würzburg,
on the Polygraphy.

I was hesitating, with wavering mind, most invincible Caesar Maximilian, and for some little while I doubted within myself whether I would be doing something worth the labor if I dedicated the volume of our Polygraphy to Your Caesarean Majesty. For among the great you are greatest, among the more powerful most powerful, and among all the wise by far the wisest; for this reason nothing ought to be inscribed to you except what is great, nothing dedicated except what has been worked out with the highest learning. But I, having attained nothing, or little, of literary learning, hesitated for some time, not without reason, whether I ought to bring my trifles before so great a Majesty, since I am neither fit in intellect to elaborate anything, nor adorned with the necessary charm of expression. For I know, and cannot deny, how scanty my equipment is, how unpolished my intellect, and that I have no vein of eloquence for speaking; and for this reason, upon just consideration, I stood back for some time, and as an unfit writer was afraid to appear

--- Page 009 ---

4
DEDICATORY LETTER

before your glorious Majesty. For since after God you are by right Lord of the world, it is no wonder if I, unlearned and the least of monks, fear the judgment of so great a Majesty as yours, and am very afraid to offer those lucubrations of mine which I know are not worthy of your hands. And while I was turning these matters over in my mind, a messenger arrived with letters sent by your Highness, in which you summoned me to Cologne, the expenses necessary for the journey having already some time before been appointed through another. But when I had read again the letters of Your Serenity, confidence soon overcame shame, and consideration of your inborn gentleness drove away fear. For I know your disposition to be most mild: since you are the most modest of all, you are accustomed both to show gentleness to the humble and to resist the proud. You do not know how to be vainly exalted, nor, in the manner of tyrants, to despise the humble who are devoted to Your Highness; for just as you have no way to increase on earth, so no envy will penetrate your mind. You are the greatest, and many things, great things too, you have accomplished no less bravely than wisely, both in peace and in war; by these you have attained an eternal name, and, amid reverence for the highest honors, you are celebrated with the love and favor of all your subjects. These are the riches of princes: the love and favor of the people, and to do justice to each one according to the commandment of the charity of God. Since, therefore,

--- Page 010 ---

LETTER 5

all men carry your name to the stars with praises, and all proclaim you most mild and most humane, there was henceforth no reason why I should suspect that you would be more severe toward me in particular, especially when by your letter you deign to encourage me to write. Animated at last, therefore, by the weighing of these things, I laid aside all fear and took up great confidence in the gentleness inborn in you. Indeed, since all draw confidence from the fountain of your piety, why should I alone be cruel to myself? Therefore, with that humility which is fitting, which I both owe and can offer, I approach the throne of your most excellent Majesty, prostrate upon the ground; I devotedly offer the aforesaid work of Polygraphy, and humbly commend my littleness to Your Serenity as a servant to his lord. Receive now, then, most clement Caesar, the very slight little gift which I bring, in which may you deign to weigh my mind, most subject to you, not its value. Now in the following system of Polygraphy there are, in certain places, some mysteries wrapped in enigmas, lest the deeper meaning of the art, which lies hidden, become manifest to the understanding of all. Just as not all things are suitable for all people, so also not all things everywhere and always suit everyone. There are therefore certain mysteries enclosed here, which my

[segment 2]
--- Page 011 ---
In my judgment, these can be more useful and more necessary to no one than to princely men. For the more matters they attend to, the greater caution of secrecy must be used in their counsels. For this book teaches princes to command secrecy over their secrets. But because Your Caesarean Majesty, occupied with many affairs, does not have time to search out the things that lie hidden, wrapped in mysteries, lest you be deprived of the fruit of my labor, I have appended a little book extracting the kernel of the whole work, which I have entitled The Key of the Polygraphia; with it as guide you will more easily understand everything shut up here in figures. May God preserve you unharmed for our salvation. I wrote on the twenty-sixth day of the month of April, in the year of Christ one thousand five hundred eight.

INDEX

--- Page 012 ---
INDEX, OR CATALOGUE OF THE LUCUBRATIONS OF JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS, FIRST ABBOT OF SPONHEIM, AND AFTERWARD OF ST. JAMES AT WURZBURG.

Johannes Trithemius, abbot, was born in the village of Trittenheim, in the diocese of Trier, on the banks of the Moselle River, on the first day of the month of February, at the eleventh hour, in the thirtieth minute after midday, in the year of the Lord’s birth one thousand four hundred sixty-two, in the tenth Roman indiction; his father was Johannes de Heydenberg, and his mother was called Elisabeth de Longouico. This was, namely, the year in which the city of Mainz, amid the quarrel of two pontiffs, was captured, lost its liberty, and found servitude, many of its citizens having been driven into exile; in the fourth current year of Pope Pius, the second of that name, and the twenty-second year of Emperor Frederick III.

In the year of the Lord one thousand four hundred eighty-two, moreover, in the monastery of Sponheim in the diocese of Mainz, on the first day of February, the very day on which he had once been born, he put off secular dress and there took up the monastic habit under the rule of the holy father Benedict, according to the observance newly introduced. In the following year, which was the eighty-third above one thousand four hundred, when Johannes de Colenhüsen, the abbot, had been transferred to Seligenstadt by canonical postulation,

--- Page 013 ---
Johannes Trithemius, by the common election of the brothers, was promoted and adorned as abbot of the aforesaid monastery of Sponheim on the fourth day before the Kalends of July, and presided for twenty-three years, one month, ten days, and nine. After these had passed, not without many labors, provoked by the rashness of certain rivals, he accepted the abbey of St. James at Würzburg, which was being offered to him, and of his own free will resigned Sponheim, full of unprofitable cares.

In both places he wrote several little works, from which he recently recorded for me in his own hand, on a sheet, the items written below. Since you, as a friend, ask me for their titles, I gladly consent to your wishes, so that from consideration of such great labors of our common friend and teacher you may have a closer memory of him. And first, indeed, I shall record the titles of those lucubrations which he wrote while he was abbot at Sponheim.

On the Matters of His Order That Were Dealt with in Provincial Chapters by Order of the Presiding Officers, one book. On the Visitation of Monks, one book. On the Manner of Holding a Provincial Chapter, one book. On the Rule of St. Benedict, two books. Homilies and Exhortatory Sermons to His Monks, two books, which he dedicated to his predecessor. On the Temptations of Cloistered Persons and Their Remedies, two books. To Nicolaus, Priest of Mernica, on the Priestly Life, one book. Against the Private Property of Monks, one book.

[segment 3]
--- Page 014 ---

*Lucubrations*, 9

*On the Vanity and Misery of This Life*, one book. A penthic work, that is, a mournful one, *On the Ruin of His Order*, one book. *On the Illustrious Men of His Order*, four books. *Praises of the Order of the Carmelite Friars*, two books. To Gerlacus, abbot of Deutz, *Praises of Manuscript Writings*, one book. To Johannes Dalburgius, bishop of Worms, *On Ecclesiastical Writers*, one book. *On the Praises of St. Anne, Grandmother of the Lord*, one book. To the questions of Rutger Sicamber, canon of Dumetis, one book. A chronicle of his monastery of Spanheim, not a small volume, one book. A chronicle or annals of the monastery of Hirsau, two large volumes. A chronicle of the succession of the dukes of Bavaria and the counts palatine, addressed to Philip, Count Palatine, prince elector, one book. The life of St. Hirmina, nun and first abbess in the horreum at Trier, one book. *On the Misery of Cloistered Prelates*, two books. *Praises of St. Andrew the Apostle*, one book. *Praises of St. Joseph, Foster-Father of the Lord*, one book. *Praises of St. Benedict the Abbot*, one book. *Praises of the Monastic Life*, one book. *Praises of True Penitence*, one book. *Praises of the Study of the Divine Scriptures*, one book. *Monologue of a Man Devoted to God to Himself*, one book. *On the Miracles of the Holy Mother Anne*, one book. To Vdalricus, canon of Cologne, on questions concerning the Gospel of John, one book. To Gundem, on questions concerning the Psalms, one book.

--- Page 015 ---

10 *Trithemius*.

For the presidents of the annual chapter of the Bursfeld observance, he abridged the statutes of the same union, one book. *The Spiritual Exercise of Monks*, one book. An epitome or breviary of the same, one book. *On Ecclesiastical Computation*, one book. *On the Illustrious Writers of Germany*, addressed to Jacob Wimpfeling of Sélestat, one book. Letters from Spanheim to various persons, four books. The orations delivered in chapters and elsewhere are twenty-four in number, making one book. He wrote sermons to his brothers of the monastery of Spanheim, forty in number and very fine, one book. Sermons to the people on feast days, one book. Several supplicatory prayers to God and the saints, one book. From the Greek language he translated into Latin Maximus the Abbot, *On the Incarnation of the Lord*; also the anathemas of St. Cyril, archbishop of Alexandria, promulgated against the heretics in the third synod of Ephesus, and certain letters of the same synod. He also wrote, for the edification of monks, a notable work which he entitled *On the Threefold Region of Cloistered Persons*, three books. He wrote certain other smaller works as well, whose titles he did not record for me. These, most beloved brother Nicholas, are the Trithemian works which, during the twenty-three years in which he was abbot, he composed at Spanheim, for the honor of God, amid cares and the continual occupation of household affairs, reading or writing by day and night whenever he was able to steal leisure for himself.

--- Page 016 ---

*Lucubrations*, 11

During the years in which he presided, he brought into the library of Spanheim a very great treasury of books in every faculty, not only by the art of printing but also in manuscripts. For when I was with him for the space of one year, in order to learn Greek and Hebrew letters from him, I counted two thousand volumes in the library, of which at least eighty had been handsomely written with the pen on parchment and paper. I often heard from his own mouth that he had spent more than two thousand florins on books. He had not received these from the substance of the monastery, which was always very slight, but since he was known and dear to many princes, he spent the gifts with which he was honored by them, for the most part, on books. For he is the greatest lover and cultivator of books, above all those whom I know living in the flesh today. It is said by many, however, that his successor, who is now abbot at Spanheim, has alienated very many volumes. In the year of the Lord 1504, when a fierce war had arisen between the interregnum and Philip, Count Palatine, Trithemius, for fear of the enemies, withdrew with his library and the possessions of the monastery to Kreuznach, the nearest town, and I likewise returned to my own affairs. But in the sixth year above five hundred and a thousand, in the month of October, Trithemius himself, spurred on by the envy of certain men, accepted the abbacy of St. James at Würzburg, and resigned that of Spanheim. There he writes various things in peace, the titles of which he recently

[segment 4]
--- Page 017 ---

*Of Trithemius.*

He recently sent me these titles when asked. To the emperor Maximilian he published a remarkable work, which he entitled *Polygraphy*, six books. To the same, the key to that same *Polygraphy*, one book. Replies to eight questions of the same emperor, one book. To the same, *On the Seven Intelligences Moving the Spheres*, one book. To Joachim, margrave of Brandenburg and prince-elector, twenty books of various questions, orations, and propositions. To the same, *On Foreknowledge Permitted and Not Permitted to a Christian*, two books. To the same prince he wrote that remarkable and laborious work, the *Steganography*, eight books, which that rash Bouillus, not understanding it, criticized most ignorantly and most falsely. To the same prince, one book of letters. To the same, *Against Sorcerers and All Arts and Superstitions Forbidden by the Church*, five books. To the same, *On the Causes and Cure of the Falling Sickness and of Sorcerers*, three books. To Jakob Trithemius, his brother, an itinerary of his life, two books. To Burkhard von Horneck, physician, *On the Peculium of Nuns*, one book. He wrote a history, or annals, *On the Origin, Kings, and Deeds of the People of the Franks and Germans*, in three large volumes, beginning the narrative from King Marcomer, who in the 433rd year before the incarnation of the Lord led the people from the borders of Scythia into Germany, to the mouths of the Rhine, down to the twenty-eighth and twenty-ninth year of the emperor Maximilian. To Lorenz, bishop of Würzburg

--- Page 018 ---

and duke of eastern Franconia, he wrote an epitome or breviary of the histories in those same three volumes, three books. To the same, *On the Dukes and Bishops of the Franks of Würzburg*, one book. *On the Miracles Shown at the Memorial of Our Lady St. Mary at Dettelbach*, two books. *On the Miracles of the Same Shown in the Nettle-Patch at Heilbronn*, six books. An apologetic work against Charles Bouelles, who slandered matters he did not understand, one book. *On the Origin and Kings of the Franks*, one book. A chronicle of his monastery, over which he now presides, of St. James at Würzburg, one book. To Albert, of the margraves of Brandenburg, archbishop of Mainz and Magdeburg, the life of the holy arch-prelate Rabanus, three books. On that same man’s translation from Mainz into Saxony, one book. To the same, the life of St. Maximus, bishop, one book. To Johann, count palatine, duke of Bavaria, and count in Spanheim, residing at Simmern, *On the Origin, Kings, and Dukes of the Bavarians, and also of the Counts Palatine and Counts of Spanheim*, four books. He has in hand a laborious work *On Demons and Profane Arts, Sorcerers, and Superstitions*, which will have twelve books, in which he drives out, confounds, and overturns all the tricks of the diabolical arts. Letters of Würzburg, which he wrote in that abbacy, four books. He also wrote many other smaller works, suited to his own devotion and that of his friends, such as offices, a rosary, a missal

--- Page 019 ---

office, and various prayers, together with proses or sequences on St. Anne, the grandmother of our savior. He wrote praises, prayers, and a rosary on St. Mary Magdalene; a rosary on St. Joseph, foster-father of the Lord; a rosary on St. Peter the Apostle; a rosary on St. John the Evangelist. At the request of Johann, archbishop of Trier, he wrote a sequence on Anne, which begins, “Jesu, of the king of heaven,” under the melody *Ave praeclara*. Another, to be sung on her feast, which begins: “Let them exult on this day.” A third, addressed to the provincial of the Carmelites at Augsburg, which begins: “Let us rejoice in honor.” A fourth on St. Hildegard, virgin, which begins thus: “Praise be to you, Lord, king of eternal glory.” A fifth on St. Rupert, duke of Bingen, which begins: “Let them exult on this day.” A sixth on St. Joseph, foster-father of the Lord, which begins thus: “Let us sing, renowned ones.” A seventh on St. Rabanus, archbishop. An eighth on the wounds of Christ, which begins thus: “Let us adore Jesus Christ; let us weep for the crucified.” A ninth on the compassion of the Blessed Mary ever Virgin, which begins thus: “Come, let us weep with devout mind, sharing the suffering of the blessed Virgin Mary,” under the melody, namely, *Ave praeclara*. A tenth on St. Joachim, father of Our Lady, which begins thus: “Let us adore the Trinity; let us praise together the unity of Father, Son, and Spirit.” An eleventh on each person’s own good angel. A twelfth for the proper mass of each priest. A thirteenth on St. Maximus, bishop of Mainz. A fourteenth on St. Martha, virgin, Christ’s hostess. A fifteenth on St. Mary Magdalene.

[segment 5]
[Page 020]
Lucubrations of Trithemius. 15

Magdalene. A sixteenth on Saint Mary of Egypt. A seventeenth on Saint James the Greater, apostle. An eighteenth on Saint Andrew, apostle. A nineteenth on Saint Benedict, abbot. A twentieth on All Saints. And for each of the named memorials and saints he composed individual new offices of the mass, conformable to divine Scripture. He wrote besides a little work of meditations and prayers on the Lord’s incarnation, nativity, life, Passion, and death, suited to devotion, in two books. The Mirror of Heavenly Doctrine, serving in no small measure for compunction of mind, one book, which begins thus: “The beginning of human salvation.” He also composed many very devout meditations and prayers on our Lord Jesus Christ, on his most holy mother, the Virgin Mary, and on various saints, with beautiful variety; together with many great other works, which all devout posterity will see and admire. These, my dearest Nicholas, are the volumes of our abbot Trithemius, which over almost thirty-two years he has composed in continuous study, apart from those which, not yet completed, are in hand on an anvil unknown to me. Farewell, from my house at Durach, on the fifth of the month of October, in the year of our salvation 1515. Johannes Duraclusius.

From the letter of Johannes Duraclusius, his disciple, to Nicolaus Hamerius of Emel.

17

Preface of Johannes Trithemius, Abbot of St. James at Wurzburg, to the Polygraphia, addressed to Maximilian I, Caesar.

We read that many wise men of old, philosophers, kings, and princes, once devised various and manifold methods by which they might safely entrust to their messengers, when they were being sent to more distant places, the secrets of counsel and whatever secret mystery might arise that had to be entrusted, so that they would be fit to carry them and secure from every attack of the wicked, lest either the simplicity of the cabalists disclose the mystery, or the innate curiosity of the Bacucei bring harm upon apostolic affairs. Great Augustus, Caesar of the Romans, among these, wishing in difficult matters to make use of the service of messengers in obscurity, changed the utterances of conceived speech from clear forms into dark ones; and by the dance of metathesis, with a fine invention, he made their barbarousness, though resisting his wishes, accord at his command. And that receptive and, as it were, perpetual order is nevertheless subject to will and to the reckoning of time; although it keeps the messengers safe on the road from the attack of the Bacucei,

--- Page 023 ---

18 Preface to

nevertheless, since it does not keep its own regulated bounds, it will make all who look upon it anxious with suspicion about the matter.

Archimedes of Syracuse, easily the foremost mathematician, fastened all around a white bird suited to his designs, bare in head and feet, to a staff cut back into the form of a tetragon; and, when the servants had been arranged in due proportion, the work of the white bird appeared changed. When this had been properly carried out, he loosed with his own hand what had been fastened; and when he had allowed it to fly away, there suddenly came about among the familiars of the inner chambers a great separation of appearances, and at once there arose the previously far too wandering monster of a tortuous monastic image, at the sight of which no one could think what it was. Nor did the dissension of the owls come to rest until the master's image had skillfully bound the magpie again with a similar tetragon. Marcus Tullius Cicero, the eloquent orator, using both talent and art, changed the minutiae of phrase into signs, and set the diligence, or character, of his will in place of the customary phrase. Indeed, in this very novelty he used such abundance that his invention could suffice for almost all things in the world. To these heralds of mysteries, moreover, the institution of the art gave a remarkable agility, so that, beyond the nature of all things, with solid steps of the feet, and not by minutiae or moments, but by whole degrees, they serve the good will of the master. A king can always use their service most safely, although not without great labor and manifest suspicion on the part of the Bacucei. But Cyprian of the Church of Carthage, most holy...

[segment 7]
--- Page 024 ---
TO MAXIMILIAN I, CAESAR. 19

Likewise, the Bishop and Martyr, wishing to provide for the usefulness of Christians, considerably enlarged the very teachings of the Ciceronian system and added what had been lacking for our practices.

Pharamund also, the forty-third after Marcomer, most powerful king of the Franks in Germany, when in the 424th year of the Lord’s nativity he powerfully entered the territories of the Gauls, bound foreign minutiae to his secrets so that everything might be done safely.

Clodius Crinitus too, Pharamund’s elder son, succeeding his father in the kingdom of the Franks, after he had violently subjected all Gaul to his rule, ordered all the Gauls, as conquered men and slaves, to be shorn and their beards shaved; but he commanded the Franks, victors and lords over the Gauls, to grow their hair and beards. Hence he was called “Crinitus.” In imitation of his father, he wrapped the mysteries of counsel in signs both his own and foreign.

Charles, surnamed the Great, the sixty-third king of the Franks after Marcomer, who was the first of the Franks to rule the Romans, with Nanno the grammarian as his teacher, wished to adorn the German language, which was his native tongue, with grammatical rules after the manner of Latin. He began an arduous work, which, however, being entangled in many affairs and forestalled by death, he did not complete. He bound bubones with various and numerous minutiae, a man greater than whom Brabant, or Namur in Germany, has not produced. To each prefect of the kingdom he gave individual bubones by art. The Northmen, having gone out from the borders of Gothia, when

--- Page 025 ---
PREFACE TO 20

they once cruelly devastated Gaul, called bubones from Greece and prepared a safe hiding place for their secrets, which today is known to few.

Our Bede, a monk in England, not ignorant of the Northmen’s deed, described many inventions of the ancients similar to these, by which they made a dark path bright with mysteries. He first commended artrology to the memory of posterity, and invented dactylogy. There were some who discerned minutiae by visible accidents; by their prudence night was turned into day, and without the service of bubones light shone in darkness. Concerning these matters, moreover, the illustrious Count Palatine, Prince Elector, formerly a requester of our steganography, received from us a manifest experiment of tested instruction.

But others, multiplying minutiae by regulated division, after the manner of barbarians overturned the patterns of nature, hiding in darkness the dances of shadows as they arose; and where the secrecy of counsel required it, they supplied cabala to the absent through diapyromata.

Great labor is involved in these things, and long exercise of the mind over many years; in this no one suddenly becomes skilled for practical use. Necessity bears all things. There were also those whom celestial harmony taught to compose foreign words, of which there was a double use for those who understood: while one suspended the sense, the other lifted the mind into the ether. God can do all things. Many tried to seek light in shadow without light, yet they could not do so at all, because the fire lies buried in ashes. Some trained

--- Page 026 ---
TO MAXIMILIAN I, CAESAR.

painters of phantoms divided one wind into two at a point; one of them, a misophos, always lurks in darkness, while the other, a lover of light, a philosophos, hates darkness, loves light, and guards the secret by means of a bubo. Again, with Bede as witness, there were some whose advancing purpose bound the wind, invisible to the eyes, to visible things, and compelled many plants, and no less stones and fruits, which lack voices, to speak with a human tongue. Why should I bring in brute creatures, when all things obey art? This secret is rightly preferred to all inventions up to now; through it the hidden mystery will take on strength for small things, and by no means cast pearls before swine. From nothing, nothing is brought back. The known multitude of visible things, if these things are prudently adapted to them, the enlightened mind of man will know and behold wonders. But to treat more fully of these matters is not part of the present purpose; since they are greater and weightier, they require another work and another time.

Matthias, the most powerful king of Hungary, a hammer harder than adamant of the cross of Christ, victorious, waged battles; with wondrous ingenuity and art he found out many things, adorned bubones with periphrasmata, kept his own mysteries, concealed secrets with manifold variety, and by manifest industry destroyed the counsels of his enemies. He preferred wisdom to gold; therefore a wise mind in a well-ordered body...

[segment 8]
--- Page 027 ---
22 Preface to

a well-ordered body; being borne upward by desire for a higher understanding, when through the likeness of the binary it has returned from the ternary into unity, thereafter it will easily rise to the denary. Pythagoras of Samos, that philosopher, warned his disciples not to forget the quaternary, in which the first perfection of counting is contained. For thus we count: one, two, three, four. Stop your mind there, for you have ten. For the denary is the head of perfection. What is eleven, except the denary and unity? What is twenty? Twice ten. What is thirty? Three times ten. What is forty? Four times ten. You have one hundred, and you have by no means departed from the quaternary. And what is one hundred, except ten times ten? In these things, beyond measure, mysteries lie hidden, and they are manifest to few.

For the sake of brevity we pass over many in silence, who, assisting nature by art, applied prudence to secrets, and by keen study brought into easy use what seemed impossible to many who did not know. A cultivated mind can advance, and one who clings to God becomes, in understanding, higher than the world. Many are the mysteries of kings, and counsels full of cares, in which confidence and faith are required; when faith is rare it harms the one who trusts, and even when doubt is present it often admits him. Therefore it would be worth the effort for all princes to be philosophers, endowed with singular wisdom, just, gentle, benevolent, and distinguished for generosity, so that they themselves would neither wish to deceive others nor easily be overthrown by others. Justice alone, the companion of prudence, will make a prince glorious. At the urging of the aforementioned prince and lord elector

--- Page 028 ---
To Emperor Maximilian I.
23

Philip, Count Palatine and Duke of Bavaria, I, Johannes Trithemius, now abbot of the monastery of St. James at Würzburg, but then of St. Martin the Bishop in Sponheim, in the diocese of Mainz, began to write eight books of mysteries in the Steganography. While I was already continuing my night-work on the third book of that same work, I am going to tell the matter briefly, if I can.

In the year of the Lord’s Nativity 1499, in the second Roman indiction, when Arnold Bostius, a Carmelite of the town of Ghent, asked me by letter what new work I was bringing forth, and with what studies I was occupied, on the second day of Palms I wrote back to him through merchants, as to a friend, that I had under the hammer a new and wonderful work of Steganography, and I reported in order what was contained in each of its books. But before my letter had reached Ghent, Bostius, in the days of Easter, passed from life to Christ. The prior of that place received the letter, opened it, read it, and, astonished, as I think, by the novelty of the promises, shared it, at the request of many others, to be read and copied.

Hence it happened that my letter itself, within a short time, was spread through almost all Germany and France, and was copied many times by many people; it brought even many most learned men to amazement and wonder, to such a degree that they hesitated as to what, in truth, they ought to think of me. Among these were some who steadfastly asserted that my promises in the said letter were frivolous, impossible, and false, shamelessly devised by me for the sake of empty glory.

[segment 9]
--- Page 029 ---

Others, however, said: “This abbot Trithemius promises great and wondrous things. If he can do them, he can do so only by the ministry of demons, since they seem to exceed by far the bounds of nature. But if he cannot do them, who will doubt that he is a liar, and deservedly to be refuted by all good and learned men?” But let both alternatives be far from me, since I promised natural things.

Of all those who rashly judged matters of mine unknown to them, the most rash, and finally the most unjust assessor of things, came into the open: Charles Bovillus, a Picard by nation. After he had traveled through the upper parts of Alsace, he came to me at Spanheim for the sake of hospitality, intending to return to France by way of Trier. I treated him with all the courtesy I could, and, for as long as he was with me, showed him every kindness of hospitality with a cheerful face.

And since, as is customary among friends, especially those devoted to literary studies, I had shown my most welcome guest all my things for him to see, among the rest there also presented itself the aforementioned work of the Steganographia, not yet completed at that time. Bovillus saw it and read it cursorily; with his mind occupied elsewhere, he said that he marveled at the invention, praised it, and did not trouble himself as to the way in which it might be understood. Hence, since he did not ask for the key of understanding, he deserved to hear or perceive none of the things contained in that work of ours.

After this he returned to France, repaid us evil for good, and shamefully violated the bond of Christian friendship. For when he was asked by our common friend lord Germain de Ganay, later bishop of Orléans, what he had found and seen at my house in Spanheim,

--- Page 030 ---

he wrote back falsehoods for truths and lies for benefits, condemning and rashly judging things that he was in no way able to grasp with his understanding. For, since he believed that the palm of learning would be awarded to him by envy and falsehood, he wrote back to the aforementioned most learned man a letter full of falsehood, lies, injuries, and many insults. In it, making mention of the Steganographia, which he had not understood, he falsely, mendaciously, and with excessively injurious rash presumption proclaims that I am devoted to depraved arts, a magician and a necromancer.

To his most mendacious injuries and blasphemies, God being merciful, I shall shortly respond in such a way that all posterity may understand both that I am innocent and that Bovillus, in this matter, is impious, cruel, rash, and a liar. I firmly affirm, I truly say, and I confidently swear upon my soul, that I have never had any dealings with demons, or with depraved and pernicious magical or necromantic arts; rather, each and every thing that I have either written or promised that I would write is pure, sound, natural, and in no respect whatever contrary to the Christian faith.

Therefore I do not fear to submit my promises to the examination of good and learned men, whenever and as often as there is need; and I in no way intend to resist their determination. There was a reasonable cause for my inserting this account of Bovillus’s rashness into this preface of mine: namely, so that all may understand that it has been done by mature counsel that the work of the Steganographia remains buried in darkness up to now, not because I fear the rash judgments of Bovillus;

[segment 10]
--- Page 031 ---

but so that I should not seem to give many similar men cause to suspect similar things about me. Indeed, so far as concerns me, I would rather appease my rivals by silence than provoke them by letters or writings to madness. For I judged it better to condemn all my marvelous discoveries to perpetual silence than to incur, even through men’s false estimation, the charge of magical or necromantic pernicious superstition. Let Steganography, therefore, lie hidden in darkness, and let it not become common to Bouillus’s mendacious company, which is accustomed to judge of matters unknown and to corrupt the reputation of a good man merely from a lust for depravity.

Nevertheless, at the urging of friends, so that our rivals may understand, along with Bouillus, how rash and lying an injury he has done us, while Steganography remains within its own bounds, we consent to publish the present work, which we call Polygraphy, divided into six books. Let those who wish read it, and let friends read this volume without resentment; when they have understood all its hidden mysteries, let them themselves, without envy, be judges whether they are natural or superstitious. For I know and am certain that all these things are sincere, pure, natural, and far removed from every pursuit of evil superstition.

But whenever the discourse proceeds rather obscurely, it is for this reason: lest a madman abuse, for destruction, the sword of defense which a man sound in mind and thought carries for his own protection. Yet nothing evil is contained in it on that account, since even good things are abused for evil by the perverse. But whoever presumes to condemn this edition of ours

--- Page 032 ---

is himself conscious and a witness that he has never truly understood it. The mysteries are concealed under enigmas, lest the secrets of the Bubones be made in any way manifest to the Bacucei. If anyone delights to know these things, let the reader approach with envy put far away.

For I am a Christian; I am a priest; under the rule of the divine father Benedict I am also a monk. I love Christ, and with whatever sincerity of mind I can, I always worship him devoutly. I have no dealings with demons, I have had none, and, with God as my protector, I shall have none; no pursuits in magical, necromantic, or profane arts; no occupations; no teachings. Whoever thinks otherwise of me thinks wrongly, does me injury, and defends Bouillus’s open falsehood. But now let us set these things aside, and let us recount in order what is contained in each book of our Polygraphy.

PINAX

--- Page 033 ---

PINAX, OR INDEX OF THE THINGS THAT ARE TREATED IN THE FIRST BOOK

In the first book of this Polygraphy of ours, we arrange the words of the Latin language, as many as are discerned, by placing them in the order of our ternary. By their use and service, all princes throughout the world will find a secure aid in any secrets whatsoever, one that will remain perpetually hidden without danger to the mystery, without mark, and without suspicion of the Bacucei. For the Bubones this road is secure, and it is to be violated by no investigation by anyone. Knowledge of the hidden matter is granted to two only, if the binary is skillfully brought back to the monad.

Ignorance is the mother of error, and he who scorns to learn the good that he does not know loves blindness. The paraphrases themselves are set out in the manner of one either warning or entreating, and they can be varied for every visible subject of this world. For they are suited to individual mysteries, and are set before the secrets of the Greeks no less than before our own. When you have understood the things you see, do not rashly despise the hidden things you do not see. You see the accidents; the substance remains invisible. Hence greater things lie hidden, lesser things are open to sight. Since this has been done according to reason, let no good man take it in an evil sense, since from these things every capable person could, if only he wished, make progress to higher matters.

[segment 11]
--- Page 034 ---

TABLE OF BOOK I.

On this occasion I have not thought fit to pour higher matters into the light, since an inopportune time seems to stand in the way. I have prefixed the letters of the Latin alphabet in order to the individual words with this consideration: that the operator may have an easy method in his undertaking, and that the reciprocal party, in reporting back, may be relieved of excessive labor. The paraphrases that are set opposite, outside the straight order of the lines, are not without the mystery of the art, since they serve light-bearing propositions for the necessary consummation of perfection, not obscure ones. For this reason they are also taken up in the former and neglected in the latter. For they themselves are certain means by which every suspicion of an adversary is removed. Everything has been set down according to reason, so that not even one proposition is introduced idly. For example, the farther the sun advances from the equinoctial circle, the more the days are lengthened for those who dwell in those regions.

Therefore, so that unity may proceed in the right order, through the ternary, into the binary, the paradigm that we hand down must be observed with greater care. And first indeed, after prior consideration, let the operator, understanding his intention, note what he is going to grant to the ternary through the binary; let him divide the day together with the night by minutes, and let him distinguish the minute divisions of the winds not by an alien but by the proper supposition of the phrase. In every mystical business let him carefully beware lest he overturn the established order of the art, or substitute one paraphrase for another, or grant to one what is owed to another; otherwise unity does not admit the binary, and the whole operation will be confused and useless. Let night not, through excessive length,

--- Page 035 ---

wander without direction; not advancing, it will stand beyond the limits appointed for it by the art. Let curiosity not change water into snow, nor let him sleep by day who keeps watch by night. Let the mind, solicitous in its intelligence, also make use of art, lest the hand, through its own lightness, commit an error, which, though slight and small in the monad, is always accustomed to appear large and very great in the binary. Only one paraphrase from any order is admitted into the service of the secret; following the example of a servant, whenever a small particle of light has demanded it of the day, one phrase likewise adhering to it is taken up, not several. These things must be commended to a vigilant mind, and care must be taken lest the wind be animated.

After this, a rule is prescribed for one approaching the practice of the art: that, insofar as he can, he should imitate by mystical art the single person of a human being, composed of two natures, namely the soul, surpassing visible things, and the flesh, remaining visible. For just as the soul is not seen, although it has been placed in a visible body, so the secret of the mind, in order that it may remain secret and nevertheless operate at will, requires the guidance of light, while it endures in its workshop of darkness. Therefore, whenever the body serves the soul, the servant the master, and the will reason, see that the principal power and virtue of the paraphrastic potency in the light is preserved in its natural vigor. For by this lesson of the Peripatetics, the first will duly enter the second, and the monad the binary.

Consequently we likewise advise the willing student that, whenever the leaf is moved by the wind, he should discern the property of each nature, compare the night itself with the day by degrees and minutes, and conform darkness to light.

--- Page 036 ---

But if Phoebus should prejudice the night, or night the sun, through which the confusion of an eclipse would arise, then either let unity take back the binary that it released in the light, or let the shadow of night be brought back to the first line of the sun. Keep fixed in memory that whatever exists anywhere has been produced in number, weight, and measure. Therefore, when you have cut into the knot of the difficulty just mentioned, count the minutes of the night; also note the paraphrases of the daylight; and so adjust one from the other that the necessary proportion may follow the action. Beware lest, during the sun's eclipse, you interrupt the necessary labor.

Pay no less attention to remembering the ancient fathers: Thuisco, who is the grandson of Noah and the father of the Teutons, and Marcomer, who is read to have been the first to lead the people of the Franks from the borders of Scythia into Germany at the mouths of the Rhine. For the Franks are brothers of the Greeks and mingled with the Germans, once making two languages from one. Hence, according to the oracle, they take up g.g. who have lost one g.

Finally, studious inquirer into Polygraphy, you are advised that after, in your desire for these inventions, you have obtained the true method of operation, you should prudently guard everything and not pour it out indiscriminately into the knowledge of all people, especially filthy ones. For this reason, do not ask, since you know that there is nothing in the world so good and holy that the wicked do not very often abuse it.

EO.

[segment 12]
--- Page 037 ---
32

AN INDEX OF THE THINGS THAT ARE TREATED IN THE SECOND BOOK OF THE POLYGRAPHIA.

In the second book of the Polygraphia, nothing new is composed beyond the first; in it only the paraphrases of the day are varied. Therefore we warn that whatever we said must be observed in that book must also be kept in this one. For the symbols of both books are arranged in the same order and sequence under the path of light, and are necessarily changed only in the meaning of the paraphrases. In both books, according to the capacity of human intelligence, multiple progressions can be made, when the words are varied by metathesis. The feronomata also are changed at will, if ever the minutes are converted into whole degrees. When this has been done, a marvelous work will arise in the art in every respect, and the world, brought forth by an image, will stand in the light, and there will be no fear at all of the bacucei in the one performing the operation. I could have given these matters a more beautiful face if I had come to writing them either induced by some hope of profit or by the use of my own advantage. But since none of these things induced me to write, it seemed enough to have given those who desire it the opportunity and the material to add more polished things to our inventions, and to beautify art by art.

EO

--- Page 038 ---
33

AN INDEX OF THE THINGS THAT ARE CONTAINED IN THE THIRD BOOK OF THE POLYGRAPHIA.

In the third book of the Polygraphia, having crossed the boundaries of our prescribed nation by a plan of reason, we are led to foreign things, and, though placed essentially in the midst of the Franks, we wander farther through images and likenesses of barbarians. For with almost four thousand mythological phrasmata arranged in order by art, we show a very easy way to artificial unity, through which it will securely communicate symbolic notions to its binary at all times. Here the binary is harder than unity, and unless our philologist is sharpened by prudence, he will make the binary contrary to the monad. For only the ternary, which proceeds from unity and binary, like a palindrome connects both in itself. But if the necessary usefulness of reason is extended beyond the limits of the proposed matter, let the paraphrases be counted by minutes from the beginning of the day, until the minutes establish the degree in which night is made equal to day. The face of this image knows perpetual silence, nor does it fear the deceits of the bacucei. It is extended to whatever abundance you wish, and is varied without number.

6

--- Page 039 ---
34

A LITTLE INDEX OR TABLE OF THE THINGS THAT ARE TREATED IN THE FOURTH BOOK.

In the fourth book of the Polygraphia we place much confidence in the binary, which, with all obstacles removed, without laborious difficulty, we have reduced to unity at first sight. Hence here we use a circle in great things, and a line or point in the smallest things. Nor does it matter if night surpasses day, or if light does not agree with darkness, because the circle receives no end, and fullness does not admit emptiness. For they are contraries, and whenever they succeed one another, one is destroyed by the other. A singular mystery conducive to the binary is handed down in this fourth book of the Polygraphia, since the airy wind itself, the composer, is never designated in anything other than the binary. But in that part the lightness of owls is always suspect to us in difficult matters. The construction of this matter makes paraphrases from infinite atoms.

EO

--- Page 040 ---
35

AN INDEX OR TABLE OF THE THINGS THAT ARE TREATED IN THE FIFTH BOOK.

In the fifth book of the Polygraphia, we have written in order, on two tables, the letters of the Latins, which we Germans also use; one of these is called the straight table, while the other is called the reversed table. Under these we have placed windy dances, so that what we seek with labor in foreign things, we may see without difficulty in our own. For besides those things that have been set down by us openly, still more secrets also lie hidden in the inner parts; through understanding them a path is opened for the operator to greater things, which nevertheless are by no means set forth for everyone. We have also subjected letters to numbers, by which hidden things may become manifest according to our will. Hence, through the skillful operation of this book, all messages are safely delivered to cabalists, although the mysteries are not free from suspicion on the part of the bacucei. For every metathesis of the form of signs generates suspicion in the beholder, although it brings no harm to the owls. But you, who have attained understanding of this work, will easily penetrate every metathesis of letters among our own people and among the Latins.

[segment 13]
--- Page 041 ---

36

A LITTLE INDEX, OR TABLE, OF THE THINGS THAT ARE CONTAINED IN THE SIXTH BOOK.

In the sixth book, which is the last book of our Polygraphy, after introducing a mental narration, we have set out in order the minute details of almost all the preceding signs and degrees, and, with arithmetical distributions, have drawn the offices of the letters into the service of cabalistic operation. One thing lies hidden here in the bark, and another is found in the common operation of the signs. For just as the spirit in the blood gives life to the human body, so the vigor hidden in the art commends the operation. Therefore, whoever needs knowledge of this invention should open the eyes of his understanding, so that he may comprehend what is the particular nature of each operation. For we desire everything to be open to good men in such a way that, if it could be done, it would nevertheless never become manifest to the reprobate. Necessity compelled us to fit the letters of the Latins to numbers, lest, if we were too obscure, the intention of our mind should be clear to no one. Yet this novelty, so that it might not be made public without reasonable cause, had to be mystically hidden under unknown signs and marks of numbering. For nocturnal winds are always stronger than daytime winds, and just as night interrupts day, so light shudders at darkness. What nature denies, no one successfully dares. We ask the reader not to despise this laborious

--- Page 042 ---

BOOK VI. 37

work of ours before he understands it, but, so that he may be a judge without rashness, let him prudently remove the bark, in order that he may reach the hidden kernel. The work is good and natural, and can be turned to a good use or a bad one, according as the desire of each person's will is moved.

A sword is a good instrument, and is turned to a good use whenever it is carried for defense without injury to one's neighbor. But if it is unjustly extended to the harming of anyone, through abuse a good thing is slackened into evil. Therefore, so that good men may have the means by which they may prudently provide for their affairs, the Polygraphy is written by us; and so that no occasion for offending may be offered to the wicked, the kernel is hidden in the bark under various enigmas. He who is good does not abuse a good thing for evil; rather, if he can, he turns evil into good.

WHAT ARE THE CAUSES OF THIS WORK OF OURS, THE POLYGRAPHY, AND AT THE SAME TIME ITS ADVANTAGES.

We undertook the labor of the present work, both rare and difficult, for no other reason than to provide for the advantage of those in need. I say those in need, not of food or drink, but of assistance in preserving mysteries without danger. We therefore judge it worthwhile,

--- Page 043 ---

38 CAUSES AND ADVANTAGES

if we briefly note the advantages and benefits that can come to those who use these inventions of ours. Indeed, since in our household affairs there are no matters such that we need these teachings for the concealment of secrets, we have surely labored for those to whose command deference was owed. The first advantage is that, once the understanding of this work has been completed, it gives an easy method by which you may safely, secretly, and without any suspicion from any observers make known the secret of your mind, by the industry of the art, as broadly as there is need, across any distance of places, in regulated speech. What lies hidden in these things the work of Steganography brings forth; it requires neither letters nor a messenger who knows the secrets he carries. It would be otherwise in matters admitting the light, according to the common custom of all our people. Do not understand the matter otherwise than it is. The second advantage is that, by the ministry of this work, in a short space of time, you will teach a man ignorant of the Latin language, provided only that he knows how to read and write letters in his mother tongue, to write in Latin as you wish, ornately or elegantly, and to conceal and hide all the secret mysteries of his will, and to communicate them to one who knows the art, as widely as he pleases, safely, secretly, and without suspicion. Again I warn you beforehand not to understand that we have promised otherwise than we do promise. The third advantage is that I shall teach a man unskilled in the Roman language, in a few hours, not only to write and read what we promise, but

[segment 14]
--- Page 044 ---

OF THE UTILITY OF THE WORK.

but also to understand it, if you prudently apply the function of the office, which we have judged should be reserved, not without reasonable cause. For the pupil of the new art will write a language he did not know, and he will understand and interpret Latin discourse composed under regulated instruction, if you yourself have sufficiently directed him word for word. Finally, this instruction can be extended to all the affairs and business of the world. If anyone wishes to learn from me how these things can be done, let him first remember to show himself such that he deserves to receive it. For not everything is owed to everyone, but one man is to be preferred to another.

The fourth utility is that, by extending the likeness of the thing received, all these things can be extended into any language whatever, once the instituting binary has been reduced to unity. Yet not just any person can do this indiscriminately, but only the one whom art and nature have dignified, with God going before by his special grace.

The fifth utility is that, by imitation of the likeness, we can communicate to one who knows the art of our invention whatever secrets of our mind you wish, however abundantly, in manifold ways, and most widely, by various methods, which cannot be known or noticed by any other person. Here there is given a manifold speech without voice, through which, safely, securely, very widely, with all suspicion removed, we shall be able to indicate fully to those who know the art all the conceptions of our mind, however difficult. And this marvelous invention has innumerable progressions,

--- Page 045 ---

OF THE CAUSE AND UTI-

and is capable of all mysteries. The sixth utility is that, through understanding this work, you will be able to discover any regulated metathesis or transposition of letters whatever, and nevertheless to form them in such a way that they serve only those who know the art, and absolutely no one else in the world. And there is great force in the form and also the operation of metathesis, which both has several modes and is varied in many ways.

The seventh utility is that, through understanding this volume, a very great faculty is given to those who desire to enlarge their memory without labor, whenever the seeds of minutiae are set in their proper degrees. For the sake of goodness, all things lie hidden, wrapped in the secrets of words and figures; if anyone desires to understand them more fully, let him recall to mind that persistent labor conquers all things. For it will have been enough for me to have discovered the principles of the art, and to have handed down a crude, and deliberately unformed, method of writing symbolically. For there was neither time nor reason for our inventions to be shaped in a more polished way, lest honey produce vomiting in the nauseated, or truth produce hatred in the indignant. Every age must be valued according to its own merits, and swine are not to be adorned with pearls, nor burdened with precious gems. Everything is untimely that does not accord with the morals and times of the mortals upon whom it is thrust. Many of the noblest talents of men have belonged to times not their own, and almost every day more new things are discovered in times not proper to them. What benefit did it bring the excellent versifier Bartolo

--- Page 046 ---

UTILITIES OF THE WORK.

Bartolo, under Pope Innocent VIII, to have had a marvelous invention of nautical art, which in a certain little book he offered for sale for one hundred thousand ducats, when neither the Supreme Pontiff himself nor the Genoese, to whom he proposed that they buy his matter, deigned to come to terms with him even for a small amount? For he promised that by his art he could keep a ship unharmed in the middle of the sea for thirty months, repair it when broken, and recall without danger anything cast into the deep. But no one cared. Nor does it escape me that many, with Naso, have perished by their own genius. Therefore it is better to set a limit to knowledge than to expose oneself fruitlessly to the hatred and detraction of many, for whom it is more familiar to despise any knowledge whatever than to have learned it.

END OF THE PREFACE, ON THE SEVENTH DAY OF APRIL, in the year of the Lord's Nativity 1508.

C S CLA-

[segment 15]
--- Page 047 ---

42

THE KEY TO THE POLYGRAPHY OF JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS, ABBOT OF SAINT JAMES OF WURZBURG, formerly of Spanheim, of the Order of Saint Benedict, of the observance of the Fathers of Bursfelde.

A Preface to the Explanation of the Whole Work of the Polygraphy of Johannes Trithemius, Abbot of Pæapolis.

No one who reads these things, as I trust, will judge me either proud or superfluous because I myself have commented on the Polygraphy recently composed by me, especially if he can weigh exactly what cause led us to speak obscurely. For whatever, if made public, can do harm had reasonably to be concealed in mystical language. But the subject matter that we treat in the Polygraphy is very good, useful, and honorable among good men for the advantage of the commonwealth, and for this reason, persuaded by reasonable cause, we wrote it: that by its service we might render good men safe and secure from the investigation of the wicked when announcing their secrets to one another.

--- Page 048 ---

PREFACE. 43

Yet lest an opportunity be offered to wicked and perverse men, who even more frequently misuse good inventions, to deceive the simpler and less cautious in their judgment of things, we hand down in it mystical, or hidden, methods of writing, and we have covered the truth lying hidden in the marrow with certain coverings of figures. Still, we do not at all doubt that any industrious man, eager with desire for these matters, will easily grasp everything if he applies even moderate persistence in searching it out. For the things I have handed down are not so difficult, nor wrapped in such great mysteries, that they cannot be thoroughly penetrated by a studious man. For everywhere we have so tempered the riddles of our diction that they are readily open to learned and studious men, and nevertheless remain inaccessible to sluggish men of darkness.

But after it pleased me to offer the work of the Polygraphy itself, dedicated to the most serene and unconquered Maximilian, Caesar of the Romans, Emperor of the world, ever Augustus, I judged it worth the effort to make it clear and open by a brief and concise interpretation, lest the mind of his sacred Majesty, which is daily stretched by many affairs and cares, be drawn back from understanding it by the darkness of the mysteries. For unless my opinion deceives me, the work will be the more pleasing and useful to his Majesty in proportion as it is shorter and clearer.

--- Page 049 ---

44 EXPOSITION

First, therefore, with the Lord granting it, we shall briefly explain the meaning of our proposal, which is contained in the Prologue wrapped beneath various riddles; then, through the individual books of the Polygraphy, we shall succinctly set forth, according to their order, the methods of operation, rules, precautions, and distinctions that remain necessary, as far as the powers of our talent allow. All these things, so that they may more quickly become accessible to those who desire to know, we shall show step by step through examples, and we shall be as brief as the matter and reason permit.

THE KEY OF THE EXPLANATION OF THE POLYGRAPHY OF JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS, ABBOT OF PÆAPOLIS.

Exposition on the Prologue.

Our entire intention in the whole work of the Polygraphy turns upon this purpose: that we might hand down fixed methods of writing secretly, through which kings and princes, nobles and commoners,

--- Page 050 ---

ON THE PROLOGUE. 45

priests and laymen, learned and unlearned alike, may safely and without any suspicion of a hidden secret announce to one another, and to any friends, even those far distant from each other, by the service of letters, whatever conception of their minds may be needed, and as often as it may be needed.

And lest we seem to have presumed to do this either first or alone, we forewarn the reader that many of the ancients long ago, especially tyrants, princes, and kings, moved by similar consideration, devised for themselves familiar and varied methods and rules of writing, by which they attempted the same thing in general. From their number, for the sake of an example of the proposed matter, we first introduced Caesar Augustus, whose hidden method of writing you should take openly in this way.

Mystery. He changed the breathings of the conceived voice from bright signs into dark ones; and by a certain beautiful invention, through the movement of metathesis, he made their resisting barbarism of sound conformable to his command.

That is: the great Caesar Augustus, in order to write secretly and safely what he wished, changed the letters of the alphabet by various transposition, so that he placed B for A, C for B, and so on for each. And so that the writing might remain more intricate and more secret, at his own judgment he transposed the letters across their breadth and length to any distance of order, number, and place.

Mystery. And this receptive order, as though perpetual and subject to the will and reckoning of time, preserves messages unviolated from the assault of [unclear].

[segment 16]
--- Page 051 ---

...preserves them, yet, not having fixed limits, it makes all who encounter it anxious with suspicion of some hidden matter.

Explanation. Although no one can easily grasp with the understanding the hidden, secret mystery under the transposition of letters, nevertheless this writing makes those into whose hands it comes suspicious about the hiding-place of the matter enclosed.

Mystery. Archimedes of Syracuse, easily the chief of mathematicians, used his white bird, suited to his established rules, naked through head and feet, etc.

Explanation. Archimedes had two rods of one length and thickness cut, round or square; he kept one for himself and entrusted the other to his absent friend. And whenever one wished to write something more secret to the other, he took the white bird, that is, a sheet of papyrus of small width but of twice the length of the rod, wrapped it around the wood, and fastened it below and above with wax. Afterwards he wrote on the sheet, joined continuously along the sides into an artful mass, whatever he wished. Then he released the sheet from the rod, and at once so great a distance appeared between the letters that not even one word cohered with another. He turned the sheet over, and, when he wished, wrote similarly on the other side. Nor was there anyone ignorant of this device who could have discovered the secret. But the man who had the other rod, when he had received the sheet, attached it and restored the letters to their former order.

Mystery. Marcus Tullius Cicero,

--- Page 052 ---

eloquent orator of the Romans, using ingenuity and art, changed the small details of phrase into signs, and the rest.

Explanation. He wrote a book of notes for his son, which the blessed martyr Cyprian later enlarged for the use of Christians, in the form, namely, of a dictionary, where the characters of his notation are first placed in order, and afterwards the words designated by those same characters, so that each word is signified by the note set before it. With these notes or characters we once saw the whole Latin Psalter, beautifully written, in the library of the major church of Strasbourg; some ignorant judge of the matter had placed on the outside this title: Psalter in the Armenian Language. But he was wrong, since it should rather have been written thus: Latin Psalter Written in Tullian Characters. For each character represents one complete Latin word. Nor does my judgment deceive me, since I possess, written in an ancient volume, the complete and uncorrupted dictionary of Cicero itself as corrected by St. Cyprian, which I have seen nowhere else. Indeed, so great is the abundance of notes or characters, with the words assigned to them, that they are altogether sufficient for writing in Latin whatever you wish.

Mystery. Pharamund, the forty-third from Marcomer, king of the Franks, when in the year 424 of the Lord’s Nativity he powerfully entered the borders of the Gauls, and the rest.

Explanation. The kings of the Franks,

--- Page 053 ---

from the time when, in the year 430 before the Nativity of Christ, they left the Scythian borders with their people and settled at the mouths of the Rhine, where Guelders now is, waged continual war with the Gauls for almost nine hundred years, and at last violently made them subject to themselves, as we wrote in the first volume of our Annals. Of these, the aforesaid King Pharamund, in order to send messengers securely, used not a few but many new alphabets, which no one understood without his voluntary instruction.

Mystery. Clodius Crinitus, son of Pharamund and after him king of the Franks, who subdued all Gaul to himself after destroying the Romans whom he found in it, used his father’s inventions in secret writings.

Explanation. This king devised alphabets of his own, new ones, and gave one to each of his leaders, while he alone retained knowledge of them all in his own possession.

Mystery. Charles, called the Great, the sixty-third king of the Franks, who first among the Franks wore the diadem of empire, and the rest.

Explanation. This man, the elder son of King Pippin, is read to have been born duke of Brabant in the county of Namur, adjoining the people of Liege, although some assert that he was born in the village of Ingelheim, not far from Mainz. He too invented various modes of writing, and, with the grammarian Nanno providing assistance, devised different alphabets, by which he communicated secrets to the governors of the kingdom.

[segment 17]
--- Page 054 ---

*On the Prologue.*

49 ...of the kingdom, in a particular way, he communicated secret matters to each one.

Mystery. The Nortmanni, having once gone out from the borders of Gothia, when they were devastating Gaul by a cruel invasion, summoned *bubones* from Greece and prepared a safe hiding-place for their secrets, and so forth.

Explanation. The Nortmanni, roaming through Gaul, as Bede the monk in England testifies, in order to write their secrets safely among themselves, substituted Greek numerical letters for Latin letters, from A, the first, up to the twenty-fourth point, such as W or *gu*, according to the custom of the Gauls. This manner of writing is altogether very fine: in it all the letters are Greek, and according to the Pelasgian order of numbering they designate our Latin letters, as A, 1; B, 2; C, 3; D, 4; E, 5; F, 6; G, 7; and so consecutively up to W, which is 24. Neither a Greek nor a Latin will understand this writing unless he has first learned the method and art of the device.

Mystery. Our Bede, a monk in England, not ignorant of the deed of the Nortmanni, described several inventions of the ancients similar to these, by which they made the path, bright with mysteries, obscure. He himself set down artrology, and so forth.

Explanation. This monk Bede, in his little book on the inventions of the ancients, reviewed many and various methods of writing and noting secretly, by which they safely announced their secrets to friends and spoke without voice to those present. He was the first of all to commit artrology and dactylogy to writing; by their use one person reveals to another, with complete safety, whatever secret he wishes, which no one else sitting by will notice,

--- Page 055 ---

50 *Explanation*

and much less understand. Bede himself taught that this same thing can be done in many other ways as well; since we have written nothing about these in the *Polygraphy*, we rightly decline to say more about them here. We long ago assigned the proper place for these difficult matters in the *Steganography*, which we are not yet thinking of publishing publicly.

And who could describe all the ancient methods of secret writing? For some had as familiar assistants in the service of the proposed art *bubones* called Philophotos, others Misophotos, and others called both into practice from the art. Again, some transposed the known letters of the common alphabet; others fashioned their own characters for themselves under the guise of barbarian letters. Again likewise, some used the service of visible letters, writing with minium or common encaustic; but others formed invisible characters at will according to their purpose.

The methods of visible characters for secret writing are exceedingly many and altogether innumerable; to mention them all is neither our desire nor within our power. Of invisible characters, however, there are three kinds more commonly used among mortals today. The first is called Dormatic, in which a certain artificial writing is made on the backs of messengers, visible only by washing with a certain liquid; no one can penetrate it who does not know the secret of the art. The second is called Hyphasmatic, by which writing is made on cloth with certain substances; when dried, it cannot be seen at all,

--- Page 056 ---

*On the Prologue.*

not at all, until it is perceived after being moistened in cold water. The third kind is called Aleotic, which is manifold and varied both in form and in material, and has almost innumerable methods of writing secretly. Its writing is done on paper or without paper, with *nemilua* and *cala*, with *raphocam*, *lapolce*, and various other things. Letters are also written and hidden in various ways and places, as under *race tabulata*, under *rofírila cubior*, in *leopi*, in *necoflas*, in *ceocali*, *locubat*, in *ratera*, in *lispilia*, and in other similar places almost infinite in number.

But because none of these things serves our present purpose, it is therefore fitting that we proceed to the matters which pertain to our subject. There is also another kind, *dactyletron*, not of writing, however, but of signs, by which one person can most subtly communicate the conception of his mind to another. This too is varied by so many differences that there is no one in the world who could enumerate all the species of so great a mystery. This is done by the principal method, and at the same time by many others descending from it, artfully, while sitting at table or in any other place whatever, very quietly and without anyone’s suspicion.

Finally, the means by which you may announce the secret of your mind without letters to someone absent, even without the knowledge of the very person who performs the office of messenger, remains assigned to the inventions of *Steganography*. For indeed, since many affairs arise for kings and princes, of which they sometimes do not trust themselves to give knowledge safely enough by ordinary letters to their deputies far away,

[segment 18]
--- Page 057 ---
52. Explanation in the Prologue.

it would be worthwhile for each of them to have some special methods of writing and communicating, by which they might very safely make their secrets known to their friends, especially in these times, when, because license to sin prevails, trustworthy fidelity is found nowhere in the world. Messengers of princes are seized everywhere; they are plundered by savage robbers, beaten, and very often killed. Nor do the impious plunderers spare even the letters of kings, sealed and closed with rings. Therefore it would be necessary for your commonwealth, O most glorious Caesar Maximilian, that you put on the skin of Hercules, brandish the club in your hands, and cleanse the world entrusted to you by God of these savage monsters. There will never be peace in the kingdom unless the brigands are removed from its midst. Whoever disturbs the peace of the kingdom shows himself to be the king's enemy. These are the robbers who beset the roads of travelers and seize others' goods; with them the earth is full today, and nowhere is the road safe for those who walk it.

A Brief Explanation of Those Things Which Seem Rather Obscure in the First Book of the Polygraphia.

Although nothing obscure is contained in the first book of the Polygraphia, nevertheless, lest its composition perhaps seem profound to simpler readers, we shall briefly explain the things that are thought more difficult. We have arranged the words of the Latin language in order, more than nine thousand of them, under nearly four hundred alphabets, in such a sequence that if from each alphabet only one word is taken, one after another, from the beginning to the end, the words, mutually cohering, will produce a fitting Latin discourse. For this will be a very convenient method of writing whatever we wish most secretly, since each word represents the letter set before it at its head. And let it not trouble you, reader, that a multitude of words in the open text contains a brief meaning in the hidden one, since in this childish arrangement of ours there lies concealed a higher method of writing, more broadly and much more secretly, whatever you wish; public reason has forbidden us to make it plain to all. But whoever is endowed with intelligence and applies the powers necessary for inquiry will find subtler things among the teachings hidden here, and will plainly understand things marvelously unknown before. Nevertheless, this open method of writing

--- Page 059 ---
54. Exposition.

the method will suffice for everything, since you can repeat it as many times as you wish, and the repeated narrative will always be varied. For if, as we have said, you take one word from the first alphabet, likewise one from the second, and so consequently one from each, and one from those inserted outside the ordered line, as far as the period or the end of the book, a Latin and sufficiently coherent discourse will arise for you. Care must be taken, however, that at one and the same time more than one word not be taken from one alphabet, and that the writer, through negligence or excessive haste, not skip over some alphabet and confuse the narrative. By "day" understand the open narrative, but by "night" the hidden and secret one, which should not be too long but brief. The examples that follow in the text, concerning the invisible soul and the body that is seen, pertain to both narratives, the hidden and the manifest. Whenever "the leaf is moved by the wind" and the rest occur, we advise that you so number the secret narrative against the open one that both may end together at the close of any given period. But if anything remains over from the secret matter, let it be enlarged with such moderation that both narratives end at one point, lest a confusion of eclipse be produced. The other things that follow in the first explanatory instruction of the Polygraphia are also implicitly referred to all the things we have said. But it must be noted that under the order of the alphabets there is no idle word; rather, each one represents the letter placed before it at the head. Perfect knowledge of this matter for the reader

[segment 19]
--- Page 060 ---

OF BOOK I. 55

...lest the reader lack complete knowledge of this matter, let us make the meaning clear by an example. Let us suppose the case that a wicked man seeks to be advanced through you with some friend of yours; but you, in order to cut off his importunate requests and yet not advance an unworthy man, decide to write rather secretly to your friend through the very petitioner, so that he may not understand: Beware of this man, because he is a thief and the worst scoundrel. Go to the first alphabet and write the word Conditor, which signifies C; in the second, clemens, which gives A; in the third, decernens, which represents V; in the fourth, mundana, which signifies E. Now in the hidden narrative you have caue, “beware.” Proceed in sequence also through the other alphabets, and such a discourse will result:

The gracious Creator, discerning worldly things, may pour seraphic loveliness upon the inexperienced, with all his saints in the heavens. Amen. The gentleness of the most glorious prince, reverently to be remembered everywhere by the saints, by the best precepts urges us, most abject sleepers, to abandon present things for reverence of the director of good things, and with all affection, according to our powers, to await the glory of perpetual rest. Therefore receive instruction, O vain mockers, so that you may be able, most blessedly adorned, to enter the most happy dances of the archangels of the rewarder of the orthodox. Rise up, therefore, from the most foul honors of this pilgrimage, since you are destined to enter, most blessedly and most holily, into the delights of the benign savior of the ages.

In this prescribed public narrative there is contained this sentence hidden in secret, namely: Beware of this man, because he is a thief and the worst scoundrel, but be silent, I beg you.

56 EXPOSITION OF BOOK I.

The same order is to be followed in all other cases whatsoever, and one must begin from the head as often as necessary, until you have manifestly enclosed the hidden narrative of your mystery. The words, however, that are placed outside the alphabetical order designate no letter and do not serve the hidden narrative, but have been inserted because of the lack of synonyms, so that the sequence of the Latin discourse might be completed. For we could not always have so many words with corresponding meaning, especially among pronouns, conjunctions, prepositions, and interjections, that we could make an alphabet in place of the twenty-four wandering letters, as is clearly evident after the eighth alphabet, where we placed two words outside the order, so that we would not leave the discourse defective. For thus we put: Cum omnibus, and thus: Cum vniuersis. Of these let the writer take two, whichever he wishes, so that the Latin discourse may be completed. Let him do likewise in other cases, whenever he finds similar ones.

We adopted the double W on account of our native speech, since in our German language we frequently need it. For anyone ignorant of the Latin language may write his hidden secret in German beneath a Latin discourse without any difficulty.
