NOW FOLLOW THE RULES OR CAUTIONS TO BE OBSERVED IN THE OPERATION OF THIS BOOK.

The first rule or caution advises you that the secret of your mind, which you wish to announce to an absent friend who knows the art and has the book of the Polygraphia, you should write as carefully as you can on a sheet, in Latin or German, or in any other language you wish that can be represented by Roman letters. Then place the sheet so written before you, so that for each individual letter of the secret you may be able to fit an appropriate word. For, as we have shown, in this practice of writing a whole word is taken. We gave an example a little earlier in the foregoing.

II. The second caution instructs you that, as has been said, you should not take more than one word at the same time from an alphabet in a single narrative of the public Latin discourse; otherwise you would both confuse the open sense and entirely draw away from understanding the mystery the friend to whom you had written.

III. Third, one must take the greatest care not to skip any alphabet through negligence, without taking from it one word fitting the secret. Likewise one must provide that, whenever one or more words occur outside the sequence of the alphabet, you take the whole upper line or the lower one, and not both.

d 5

[segment 20]
--- Page 063 ---

58
CAUTIONS

IV. Fourth, care must be taken that whenever the discourse of the open speech has been brought to a complete sentence, the following sentence should begin with a larger, thickened letter, according to the form of the preceding example, in which, according to the sense of the Latin discourse, we have used large letters at the beginning and small ones for the rest, in the common manner.

When the secret of your mind is lengthy, so that it exceeds the narrative of the open discourse, if much remains, you should begin again from the start; if little remains, let the discourse be recalled at will from any place. For no one even moderately skilled in this technique will be unable to know where you have begun again. Nor is there need to fear the correspondence of the discourse, for the manifest narrative will always be varied together with the hidden one, and whenever a beginning is made from the start, a new appearance of speech will follow. The continuation can also be made from the end of the first book through the second.

V. Fifth, we advise that whenever you wish to write by this art in an important matter, prepare for yourself a small booklet in which you may write your secret mystery in manifest discourse. Whoever sees or reads this booklet will suppose that no secret lies hidden, nor, even if he has suspicion, will he be able to discover any of the things you write.

VI. Sixth, it should be noted that if you transpose the order of the words throughout the whole Polygraphy in each individual alphabet by changing it through metathesis, and if you write two volumes according to that transposition, one for yourself and the other for any friend, you will write between yourselves whatever you wish most securely, and no one lives on earth who could penetrate your secrets.

--- Page 064 ---

OF THE OPERATION. 59

This transposition, moreover, can be made in infinite ways.

VII. Seventh, we warn you in advance that one must write polygraphically in one way to a man ignorant of the Latin language, and in another to one skilled in it. For the former, all the words must be written out completely, without abbreviation, distinct, complete, fair, and wholly legible, without partition, without division, and without confusion, lest, deceived by an error, he think one word is two, or suppose two words to be one. The things set down must be heeded.

VIII. Eighth, care must be taken that the person to whom you write mystically by this art has a book in every respect like your own; otherwise you would write in vain and fruitlessly to someone who does not understand.

IX. Ninth, if you wish this technique to remain forever secret and hidden, rewrite the Polygraphy anew, but do not place the alphabet letters before the words, and no one will easily suspect the method of the secret. But so that no difficulty may occur for the person writing and reading the mystery, let the letters of the alphabet be written on a sheet in the order corresponding to the words, and let that sheet be fitted movably to their headings. The letters may also be engraved on a copper plate, so that they can be moved from one order into another.

X. Tenth, it should be known that although we do not use the Pythagorean Y in German and Latin words, we have nevertheless admitted it on account of the Greek language, so that we might have a doubled twelve. Likewise we have accepted the doubled W as the twenty-fourth letter, which is wholly necessary for us in the German language.

XI. Eleventh, let the person who receives letters written from the Polygraphy to be read know

--- Page 065 ---

60 CAUTIONS

that understanding is needed in the reading. And therefore, whenever he receives a sheet or booklet written polygraphically, let him go to a secret place and, with the book of the Polygraphy opened, which he must have with him, let him examine each word throughout all the alphabets, and let him most diligently note with chalk on the table, or with ink on paper, what letter each one represents. And when he has inspected the whole letter in this way, let him compose the letters into words, and he will easily understand what lay hidden in concealment.

XII. Twelfth, kings and princes who have many deputies in different places should note that it is not prudent to write to all of them with one kind of hidden narrative. Therefore, so that they may have for each person his own secret mode of writing, let them change the order of the words as many times as they wish to have separate modes. Thus they themselves will possess the modes of writing of all, and none of their subjects will understand the mode of another. For the variable modes of writing more secretly by this art are infinite.

XIII. Thirteenth, it should be noted that, in order to stop the mouths of those who speak wickedly against the possible truth of this new invention of ours, we have placed the words in the alphabets of the first book so that they cohere with one another and produce a fitting and Latin prayer in the manner of an exhortation and supplication offered to God. Beneath whose hidden narrative [unclear], and to write in such a way to a friend skilled in the art that, if all the men of the whole world

OF THE OPERATION.

people were to come together into one place, they could never discover the secret lying hidden beneath the open discourse, provided it has been rightly arranged.

XIV. Fourteenth, it must be known that in this very invention of our Polygraphia certain things still lie hidden, far better than those already described: namely, how in a few days, not to say hours, you will be able to instruct someone ignorant of the Latin language so that he knows how to write, read, speak, and understand Latin, not indeed all things universally, but in a certain manner whatever occurs to him as necessary concerning any matter in his affairs. Likewise, how you may make known securely, safely, and most secretly to your friend far away, who knows the practice of the art, all the conceptions of your mind, without writings and with the messenger unaware; even sometimes without a messenger, naturally and without any superstition, danger, or fear. But let these things remain buried in their hiding places with the rest. This reason has moved us: because it neither pleases me nor is at all useful to cast precious stones to the wind and to undertake difficult things without the fruit of gratitude. Finally, beneath these things lie no small mysteries of subtlety concerning medicines, herbs, plants, precious stones, rivers, the members of the human body, brute animals, saints, nations, cosmography, astronomy and the seven liberal arts, and also concerning all things that are in human use, and concerning the universal world, both visible and invisible. And, lest we be more prolix than is right, all things that are in the world, by extension of the likeness of the art of Polygraphia previously shown, serve a mystical instruction.

EXPLANATION

XV. Fifteenth, note that all these inventions which we have handed down in the Polygraphia are certain small introductions in comparison with those that still remain to be discovered and written in this same faculty; concerning them we pass over in silence, lest we seem to boast in vain. For we have given suitable minds a cause for discovering greater things than we have either written or perhaps were able to discover. Let him who can add, for the completion of this invention, those things which he knows to be useful; but let him who cannot do so not rashly judge what he does not understand.

AN EXPLANATION OF THOSE THINGS WHICH SEEMED MORE OBSCURE IN THE SECOND BOOK OF THE POLYGRAPHIA.

In the second book of the Polygraphia, apart from the order and sequence of the words, nothing new is handed down from the first; therefore one and the same manner of operating will apply in both, and we carefully admonish that everything which we said must be observed in the first must also be observed in the second. A manifest difference is seen to arise only in the narrative of the prayer, since the former is composed concerning our Lord, the Son of God, while this one is raised to his mother, the ever-intact Virgin.

--- Page 068 ---

BOOK II.

In the second book there are three hundred and eight alphabets set before the words in order, though under different subjects. For from the beginning the narrative of the discourse is directed to our Lady herself; afterward the Lord’s Prayer is expanded under twenty-four alphabets; then the Angelic Salutation is added, next the Salve Regina, and lastly a brief prayer concerning Saint Anne, mother of the Virgin Mary, Mother of God. In all the books of the Polygraphia care must be taken that one word not be repeated in any alphabetical order, that is, not be placed twice, and that none be omitted, because it would cause great confusion for the reader. Therefore the utmost care must be taken that the copies be in every respect emended and corrected exactly, lest, when writing, you fall into error. But if, through the negligence of the scribe or the carelessness of the printer, you find some word duplicated under one alphabet, erase the last one and put in its place another suitable one which has not occurred earlier in the same alphabet. Know also that these two books of our Polygraphia serve sufficiently for the promised operation: by means of them you will not only be able at all times to write and announce to friends whatever you wish, very safely and very securely, but also, without any suspicion, to make one wholly ignorant of the Latin language, provided he knows how to form letters, a participant in and learned in this art, something which we believe was previously unheard of in such a manner. But since this manner of writing secretly, under a long narrative of open discourse, comprises the sentences of the hidden intention

[segment 22]
--- Page 069 ---

64. EXPLANATION

contains fewer. For that reason perhaps it will be useful only in great and difficult matters, especially among those who like leisure and dislike labor. Therefore in the following four books we shall provide easier methods of writing, though they are not entirely free from the suspicion of those who look at them, because they offend the understanding of most people. But in the Steganographia there are richer methods of writing most secretly and without any suspicion, where one word does not represent a letter, but each word represents another whole word suitable to the mystery.

Let us give an example of this. If you wish to forewarn a friend about some imminent danger under such a narrative of words as: “Melancius, Tyberius, Johannes, and Petrus, servants of the Count of Asoto, have conspired for your death, and on the fourth day after Lawrence’s day, in the night, they will surround your house; take thought what you should do.” Steganography disguises this secret as follows: “I ask you, dearest friend, to send me by the bearer of the present letter ten florins as a loan, because they are very necessary to me for the construction of a certain building; I shall faithfully restore them to you.” Who would believe or suspect that this secret lies hidden in the discourse written above?

Let us put the same thing also under another narrative: “Lord Jesus Christ, Son of the living God, who willed to become man, to suffer, and to die for us, have mercy on me, an unworthy sinner, now and in the terrible hour of my death.” See, now you have one hidden meaning of your heart veiled, within the bounds of steganography, under two narratives, in speech brief but faithful,

--- Page 070 ---

BOOK II.
65

which the whole world will not even be able to suspect, nor, if it searched for a hundred years, to find in any way, even if all the learned were to come together. Finally, the aforesaid Steganographia teaches how to hide this very secret meaning of your heart not only in one or two of the narratives written above, but in almost infinite ways, and to communicate whatever you wish most safely, most secretly, and most securely. All methods of this kind of invention can also be changed by transposition in infinite turns, and through them all things can be written and announced. But why the work of the Steganographia remains buried in silence has been said sufficiently at the beginning of our Polygraphia, and therefore it did not need to be repeated here.

Let it lie hidden, therefore, lest it be seen by the ungrateful.

EXPLANATION OF THE OBSCURE MATTERS CONTAINED IN THE THIRD BOOK OF OUR POLYGRAPHY.

In the third book of the Polygraphy we handed down a newly devised method of writing secretly, certainly quite safe and secure, but one that makes all who look at it suspect some hidden secret. For no one, however learned,

--- Page 071 ---

66. EXPLANATION

will find the hidden mystery without experience of the system, since indeed he will not even be able to understand the open writing. We have arranged fabricated words, containing nothing in themselves but merely representing the letters of the alphabet set before them; through these you will be able to write and make known all the secrets of your mind to a friend, however far absent, with the greatest security. But those unskilled in the art and ignorant of it, when they see the writing, will think it is some foreign language, and will be wholly ignorant of what it contains.

But when you wish to write or communicate something to an absent person by the method of this book, take care first of all that you properly distinguish each and every word from the others, so that each one can be very clearly discerned from another, lest the person to whom you write take one for two, or two for one, because that would produce a great error. Let us set down an example of this matter: Abrin madon badis, cadulur pasu, adin loroff masara damis, boda omo dromidrus vares mastron phosat, galos, prosol fane lemoros, aro soca bilneso, hamol heliet rhamil festa doron hubor rodex.

In this narration of invented discourse there is contained this brief secret message of a friend warning a friend about danger: “Do not come to court, because the king is angry with you.” You see how easy, safe, and secure this method is of writing and communicating whatever you wish to a friend who understands it. In all things, however, you must know that care must be taken, as was said before, that you take no more than one word from each alphabet, nor again skip any alphabet. Whenever the narra-

When you have carried the narrative of the fabricated speech to the end of the book, while the secret of your mind has not yet been completed, begin again from the beginning, and do this as often as it is necessary. Nevertheless anyone may, at his own discretion, change, alter, improve these words of ours, and add more, provided that you avoid a consonance that breaks out awkwardly into spoken sounds.

EXPLANATION OF THE FOURTH BOOK OF OUR POLYGRAPHIA.

In the fourth book of the Polygraphia, in a similar way, we have placed words devised by us in alphabetical order, but in one point alone we deliberately differ from the preceding book. For in the preceding book the words contain nothing else in themselves by way of proper meaning, except that they represent the letters placed before them at the head. But in the fourth book, which we now have in hand, each word contains its own force and nevertheless designates the letters placed before it. They have within what they display without, because the second letter of each word always serves the hidden mystery, as we shall make clear in the following example: Ecorach hanafar dulay femalas, chorion furiel anephor scabaras busarat vimalo tropen tumelech amasar. To understand this mystery, take only the second letter from each word,

--- Page 073 ---

68 EXPLANATION OF BOOK IV.

and you will find this hidden meaning: Beware this man. For the second letter in Ecorach is C, in hanafar A, in dulay V, in femalas E. Join these four letters, and they will make this word: Cave, “Beware.” Thus one should proceed through the rest to the end of the book; and if anything of the hidden mystery remains to be written, one should return to the beginning as often as necessary, until the secret has been completed in full.

In this very secret method of writing, if anyone has been trained by long practice, he does not need the book, but will readily compose, on the spot, words suited to his purpose. But anyone less practiced in composition necessarily requires a copy of the book from which to write. A copy of the volume will not be necessary for the reader, since without the book he will easily be able to find the hidden secret by means of the second letters everywhere.

Yet in great, difficult, or dangerous matters, this method of writing should not be trusted as entirely safe, because the diligence of a skilled man might by chance find the hidden mystery. This method of writing can also be changed by an intelligent man, so that the significant letters are placed in the third or fourth position of the word, with the words altered.

A BRIEF EXPLANATION OF THE FIFTH BOOK OF OUR POLYGRAPHIA.

In the fifth book of the Polygraphia we have handed down various methods of writing secretly by transposition of common letters, arranged in such an order that under their covering we shall be able to write and announce everything safely to an absent friend. Now, to speak in our own way, the progression of the transposition of letters is almost infinite, since countless things arise from one alphabet by metathesis.

First we described a straight table containing twenty-four alphabets; through understanding it, as many alphabets can be composed as there are stars counted in the firmament of heaven. For as many characters as there are in that table, so many times there arise by the art, in order, ten hundred thousand alphabets. After this we set out a reversed table distributively, which will arise as often into another table as we have changed the first letter at the head. The first letter in the first straight table is B, and in the reversed table Z. As often as you put any other changed letter in their place, so often you will find a table new in every respect, and so on to infinity. Then we expanded the first straight table, placing at the head of each transposed black letter, in minium, the letter that it represents, so that

[segment 24]
--- Page 075 ---

70 Explanation

Now this method of writing is as follows: in the first black alphabet, take one letter of the secret sentence; from the second, another; from the third, a third; and so successively to the end. When you have reached it, remember that you must return to the first order as often as necessary, until by concealing it you have completed the secret mystery of your mind. But so that you may see the order, let us set down an example. Hxpf gfhmczfueib gmbt gx hsr egerbd qopma wvz. fxeqk ak tnr qxyz. The meaning of this mystic discourse is: Beware this man, because he is evil, a thief, a deceiver, a liar, and unjust. You now see, reader, what a marvelous transposition of the letters of the alphabet this table produces, since there is no one who, without knowledge of it, could penetrate this secret. For this method of writing surpasses every common transposition of letters, since each letter is always changed from one series of the alphabet into another. From the reverse table also, which we expanded in order by a similar distribution, let us set down this example by way of introduction. Rdkt, ftz nyb, teuqz, fu zf, fd. drgh, vfd. Its hidden meaning is this: Beware this man, because he is evil. And note that under the example of the straight table already given we carried the hidden series through from the beginning throughout it, and then continued likewise through the reverse table; and again we make a circle, as you see, to the beginning of the straight table. Hence it must be considered that one series of writing can be carried through both tables, just as we have already done in the demonstration of the first example,

--- Page 076 ---

Book V. 71

or, if you prefer, through one alone, as we have sufficiently shown in the reverse table. Nor will it create difficulty for the reader to whom you send your hidden message; he will easily understand everything if he has had even a little practice in the art. The strongest proof, moreover, which increases knowledge, is frequent use in any art. After the expansion of the tables there follow certain orchemata, in which there is not only a transposition of all the letters, but also an ordered transition of one over another. For orchema in Greek is called transitio in Latin. And first we marked the leap of the letters in expanded form; afterward we reduced it into an orchema. In the operation of this orchema, a marvelous leap occurs. There are twenty-four alphabets in it. In the first no letter comes between. In the second, one. In the third, two. In the fourth, three. In the fifth, four. In the sixth, five. In the seventh, six. In the eighth, seven. In the ninth, eight. In the tenth, nine. In the eleventh, finally, ten; and so successively up to the twenty-fourth alphabet, in which twenty-three letters come between; afterward a return of likeness occurs to the beginning. The method in the orchemata for writing a mystery is similar to that which is observed in the tables of transposition of letters: namely, when you are going to write according to the rule of the orchema, you take only one black letter from each alphabet, which the letter written in red at the head indicates. For understanding this, let us set down an example.

--- Page 077 ---

72 Explanation of Book V

Prdh. ifrqyn. bdyen. zhkt. sxsol. inq. Its meaning is this: Beware this man, because he is evil. When, in writing, you have reached the end of the orchemata, and the begun secret of your heart has not yet been completed, you should know that you must return to the beginning as often as necessary, until the mystery has been completed in every respect. And know that from one orchema of letters as many alphabets are composed as the diligent investigator may wish to have. Finally, this method of writing by orchemata, that is, by transitions and leaps, is the safest of all, because there arises a transposition of letters that is not uniform, but varied in many ways. And it should be noted that true and perfect knowledge of the tables and orchemata, if you are diligent and studious, gives understanding of all writings that are written out in any way by transposition of letters; and it is a great secret, which few know: to be able to discover all meanings concealed in any transposition of letters whatever.

BRIEF EXPLANATION OF THE SIXTH AND LAST BOOK OF THE POLYGRAPHY.

In the sixth and last book of the Polygraphy we have gathered together certain characters of the ancients, which they used in place of letters, so that if anyone wishes to adopt from them an alphabet of his own, he may be able to do so, although a person of moderate skill, if he wishes, could devise more beautiful characters for himself. All the systems of this book are sufficiently open and clear, and therefore they need no explanation, or only a brief one. Yet those things which appear there as newly invented by us require a definite explanation, lest perhaps they seem to have been set down without reason.

First of all, we have made all the letters of the Latins numerical, after the manner of the Hebrews and the Greeks, thinking it shameful for us if we should be found inferior to them in this respect. For what of the fact that we have doubled certain letters, so that they might suffice for an infinite number, when the Jews, driven by the same necessity, long ago did the very same thing? And the Greeks, because of a deficiency of letters, admitted certain signs into their numbering, since they could not double the letters. But since we are pressed by an equal lack of letters, we have admitted two signs, namely this one, &, which our scribes put for “and”; and it does not matter whether it is written thus, &, or thus, et, provided that it represents six in number.

[Page 079]
74 EXPLANATION

We have added the other sign, 9, which our scribes write in abbreviation for con and com indiscriminately; here, in the order of numbers, it represents six hundred. By the same necessity we have also doubled two letters, namely r, z̃, and s, of which r signifies ninety, while the curved 2 signifies one hundred, long ſ two hundred, and closed s three hundred. Moreover, the doubled w, which we placed in the order of the alphabet because of the nature of the German language, represents the last letter, and in the series of numbers one thousand.

Let us set down a tabular example of this new invention of ours for the reckoning of numbers, so that anyone who wishes to know the method may have a definite guide before his eyes. For what has always been easy for both the Hebrews and the Greeks will not be impossible for us. The table of numbers follows.

76 EXPLANATION

We have shown a sufficient method of numbering by Latin letters, in the manner of the Hebrews and the Greeks, which we have set here, by way of digression, in a reasoned place, although it is also found earlier in the sixth book. And by the fact that, as we proceeded, we have here brought our number to a close at thirty times a hundred times a hundred thousand, we plainly declare that it can be extended to infinity. If this method of numbers seems difficult to anyone, he will easily be able to devise a better one.

Also, in imitation of the Nortmanni, from the numerical letters prescribed above we have arranged a new alphabet, by which you will be able safely to write and announce to an absent friend any secret whatever you wish. This method of writing is indeed as secret as it is new, and before us never seen or heard of; so that it seems to me wholly impossible that it could be understood by anyone without the instruction of a teacher. But now let us set down its alphabet.

 a  a   1      f  g   7      ic n  13      ih t  19
 b  b   2      g  h   8      id o  14      k  u  20
 c  c   3      h  i   9      ie p  15      ka x  21
 d  d   4      i  k  10      ie q  16      kb y  22
 e  e   5      ia l  11      if r  17      kc z  23
 et f   6      ib m  12      ig s  18      kd w  24

You now have here, behold, an alphabet made from Roman numerical letters in imitation of the Nortmanni; with it you will be able very safely to write and report to an absent friend

--- Page 082 ---

BOOK VI. 77

whatever you wish, and no one will be able to penetrate your hidden secret except the one who knows the secret of the art. For all who see the writing will think that the letters have been transposed, and they will labor in vain in reckoning them up. Let us set down an example:

laaibid ifeib, ieifeigeicckhib, cakeibid. i et kabb. gidbhid. eigih ieeigigh ibkig. & kif. iaaibifhid ibeicdaka. eih. icei et kaib.

The hidden meaning of this writing is as follows: Beware the bearer of the present letters, because he is a very wicked man, a thief, a robber, a liar, and worthless.

From the demonstration of this example it is easily given to be understood how safe and secure this method of writing is, universally, in every language known to the writer, and in every cause or business, however difficult, secret, important, or lengthy. Nor do I think that there is any person living in the world who, by his own wit and without a teacher, could read this writing. For all who see it will think it nothing other than that the letters have been transposed; and when they cannot attain an understanding of them, finally wearied by despair, they will cease from investigation, and whatever you have written will remain hidden. For no one, as I think, will weigh out this technique of writing secretly by his own powers as it actually is, unless he has either the Polygraphia, or this key to it, at hand, or has been instructed by another.

From these numerical letters, moreover, many other alphabets can be given according to the order of numbering, when the beginning for writing alphabetically is taken not from unity, but

--- Page 083 ---

78 EXPLANATION

from any other following number. But in that progression you should know that care must be taken that, to represent one letter, no more than two numerical letters be taken. Hence, when you have arrived at 1000, that is, ten times one hundred thousand, stop your course, and do not wander any further, lest the multitude of characters confuse the beauty of the writing by spoiling it. For, with unity excluded and the characters up to ten times one hundred thousand included, they will suffice for nearly thirty individual alphabets. Nevertheless, the method of the first alphabet, beginning with unity, is more beautiful for wrapping secrets in writing, insofar as it is shorter and simpler. In short, it makes the writing less tortuous.

We have also arranged another alphabet, with the Latin letters distributed in a confused order and transposed without a rule. In imitation of it, others too can be made almost without number; and now its likeness is set down.

 o  a    t  g    c  n    q  u
 p  b    h  i    k  x    y  z
 q  c    w  l    r  d    f  s
 i  e    x  m    g  z    v  w

Let us set down an example of this alphabet as well, so that we may prove that a secret can lie hidden in it:

Gquog, ozuqi, ykar, din, vc, qacquxua, riqdik v99i, aqqurixrlz, skci,

--- Page 084 ---

BOOK VI. 79

skci, idta, ykocus aqukg, vc, coddotacuorum, osoqui, iukg, ciqidiog.

This writing contains in itself the following sense: Know, friend, that the king has decreed in council that you are to be killed. Therefore flee as quickly as possible into Narbonese Gaul from before his face, lest you perish.

This method of writing too is tortuous and is subject to no regular rule, since it can be varied and changed at the writer’s pleasure by chance transposition as much as anyone wishes.

Next it should be noted that at the end of the sixth book of the Polygraphia we handed down another method of writing by numerical signs, which people commonly call ciphers; under its covering you may safely write to an absent friend whatever you wish, and no one ignorant of the art will be able either to discover or to suspect that a secret lies hidden, especially if the rules which we have given on these matters are observed with diligent caution. This is that the sheet should be formed in the manner of an astronomical table, as though it contained something pertaining to the reckoning of the stars; that is, in such a way that the characters of signs or planets are inserted, so that anyone into whose hands it may perhaps fall by chance, being led on by the likeness of a table, will not think that any secret lies hidden in it, but will suppose that everything pertains to some reckoning of astronomy.

We have arranged twelve alphabets according to the number twenty-four, assigning them in place of letters. The first of these, that of Aries, begins from unity and ends at twenty-four. We advise, however, in important matters [unclear]

[segment 29]
--- Page 085 ---

*Explanation.*

in great and difficult matters one must beware of beginning from one, because that very method of writing, precisely on account of that, may easily be suspected. But whoever wishes to use his own natural industry prudently will find subtler things from ours, and what we have handed down not sufficiently polished he will easily make better. In the numerical orchema great secrets lie hidden; if anyone properly understands it, he will penetrate many mysteries of numbers. For we had neither the time nor the reason on this occasion to pour everything out into public view; but in our judgment what we have done will be enough for the instruction of those to whom our discoveries will someday be pleasing, so that they may have many methods not only for writing secretly whatever they wish to absent partners in their industry, but also a way of producing one method into several better and subtler ones, according to each person’s capacity of understanding.

Likewise, since the numerical orchemata are sufficiently open and clear in their own places, together with their expanded tables, it was not necessary here also to repeat what has already been said, lest excessive length produce weariness for the reader. Nor did I think it necessary to set down an example of more secret writing by means of numerical figures, since there is no difficulty in understanding the method for all who possess even moderate talent. With similar ease the most ancient signs of numbering, which we have brought back into the light, will be able to be understood and grasped; by them the knowledge of numbers is extended to infinity. For whenever a small stroke is placed over a smaller number, a thousandfold is designated. For

--- Page 086 ---

*Book VI.*

X with a stroke above means ten thousand; C with a stroke above, one hundred thousand; M with a stroke above, one million; X with a stroke above, ten times one million; C with a stroke above, one hundred times one million; M with a stroke above, one thousand times one million; and so consequently as far and as much as you wish to advance in numbering, without limit.

END OF THE KEY TO THE POLYGRAPHY OF JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS, ABBOT OF PEAPOLIS, TO MAXIMILIAN I, CAESAR.

THE APOLOGY OF JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS, PREFIXED TO THE STEGANOGRAPHY.

It is the opinion of many that the ancient philosophers and sages of art and nature, if they had discovered any secrets, concealed them by various methods and figures, lest they come to the knowledge of wicked men. The more learned among the Jews confirm that Moses also, the most famous leader of the Israelite people, covered ineffable secrets of mysteries with simple words in his description of the creation of heaven and earth. Saint Jerome too, one of the most learned among our writers, says that almost as many mysteries lie hidden in the Apocalypse as there are words. I pass over the sages of the Greeks, men of no small reputation and glory among their own people, who, devoting their effort to writing fables, by prudent invention handed down one thing to the unlearned and another to learned men in the sequence of a single narrative. These most zealous lovers of wisdom, even if because of the slowness of my talent I cannot perfectly imitate them, I nevertheless judge it beautiful and glorious to admire, and with whatever diligence I can to read and venerate them. Accordingly, when I consider that they discovered great things beyond other men by their own study, I compel myself, imposing a certain force upon myself, toward at least some imitation of them, even among the very latest

[segment 30]
--- Page 088 ---

APOLOGY.
83

of them. Nor, as I think, has my opinion altogether deceived me, since through the continual study of reading I have learned many things that I did not know before, and have opened for my thoughts an entrance to investigating more secret matters. For although I am not of such great learning or industry that I would dare profess that I have grasped that method of the ancient sages for concealing mysteries, nevertheless I have devised certain methods, not wholly to be despised, as it seems to me, of writing and concealing, secretly and entirely without any suspicion, whatever you wish. At the request of the most serene prince, lord Philip, Count Palatine of the Rhine, Duke of Bavaria, and Prince Elector of the Roman Empire, the wisest patron of philosophers, than whom no one seemed to me more worthy for this great secret to be revealed to him, I have committed these methods to writing and brought them together in the following volume, not without immense labor, lest this great secret come to the ears of common charlatans or wicked men.

I ask you, reader, whoever you may be in the future, to remember how many evils in the commonwealth could be committed by wicked and wanton men through this science, if it came to the knowledge of many: adulteries, fornications, conspiracies, betrayals, murders, robberies, and countless evils. For just as a good and honorable man can, whenever he wishes, safely and without any suspicion from anyone, make known and express the secret of his will to another who knows this art, for private or common advantage, so every perverse, wanton, and malicious man, once every obstacle has been entirely removed, will be able to disclose his evil desire, however shamefully suited to his pleasure,

--- Page 089 ---

84 APOLOGY

without any suspicion. Nor could fidelity between married persons thereafter remain secure, since a woman, although hitherto ignorant of the Latin language, yet made thoroughly learned in effect through holy and chaste words of any language or idiom, could very fully understand the evil and shameless intention of her lover, even while her husband presents and praises the letters, and could in the same way send back to him, quite safely whenever she wished, her own desire at length and in sufficiently eloquent speech.

Indeed, although this science may seem childish to some, nevertheless, if it were made public, the whole order of the commonwealth would be disturbed, public trust would perish, letters, documents, writings, and finally all the very conversations of men would forever be turned into suspicion. No one would any longer trust letters, however good, honorable, and holy; no one would put faith in a letter, because, however holy and chaste the words might be, deceit, fraud, and evil deception would always be thought to lie beneath them. Men would become fearful in all things, and no less suspicious of mutual conversations than of any letters whatever. Nor could anyone ever become so learned and experienced in this science itself that there would not remain infinitely many ways, sufficient for all purposes, of writing secretly and most hiddenly, which he himself had not yet grasped. For this science is a chaos of infinite magnitude, which no one can comprehend. For however learned you may be in

--- Page 090 ---

TRITHEMIUS.
85

this art, what you understand is nevertheless less than what you do not know. For it is the property of this science always to make the disciple, provided he is industrious and willing, incomparably more learned than the master.

86 *Exposition.*

of a letter of Augustus to his son, in which he writes thus: Since innumerable matters continually arise which one of us ought to write to the other, and which ought to be kept secret, let us have signs between us, if you wish, of this kind: whenever something has to be written by us, let us write the next letter in place of each letter, in this way: b for a, c for b, and then the rest in the same manner. But for the letter z one must return to double a a.

*Archimedes of Syracuse.* Similar to this is what is read in Gellius, book 17, chapter 9. The ancient Lacedaemonians, when they wished to disguise and conceal letters sent officially to their commanders, lest, if they were intercepted by the enemy, their plans should become known, used to send letters made in the following manner. There were two rods, round, rather long, of equal thickness and the same length, scraped smooth and finished alike. One was given to the commander setting out for war; the other the magistrates kept at home, with legal authority and with a seal. When there was need of more secret letters, they wrapped around that rod a thong of moderate thinness and length, or as much as the matter required, in a simple round winding, so that the joined edges of the thong being wrapped would meet everywhere and adhere. Then they wrote the letters on that thong across the transverse edges of the joins, in lines running from top to bottom. When the thong had been written through in this way, they unwound it from the rod and sent it to the commander who knew of that device. The unrolling of the thong also made the letters broken and mutilated, and the parts and tips of them were scattered into the most different places.

--- Page 092 ---

*Exposition.* 87

Therefore, if that thong had fallen into the hands of the enemy, they could infer nothing at all from it. But when the man to whom it had been sent received it, he took the matching rod which he had, wrapped it around to the end, just as he knew it had to be done, and thus the letters, coming together along the same circuit of the rod, joined again and made the letter whole, uncorrupted, and easy to read. The Lacedaemonians call this kind of letter a *scytale*. See also Plutarch in the *Life of Lysander*.

There is also another no less ingenious method of hiding letters, described by Girolamo Cardano in the book *On Subtleties*. Take two equal sheets of parchment ruled for writing, and in the very lines make small holes scattered in corresponding places in each, yet proportioned to the size or height of the letters. Some holes will contain seven letters, some three, some eight or ten, so that all the holes together will contain one hundred and twenty letters, joining together all that can be written in them. You will give one of these sheets to the person to whom you wish to write. But when there is need, first write your message as briefly as you can, so that the message contains a smaller number of letters than the spaces can hold. Then, on the parchment placed beneath the holes, write that message; and likewise in another and another place. Finally fill the spaces of the first parchment by completing, deleting, and filling out sentences, until the sense

--- Page 093 ---

*Exposition.*

stands together. You will complete this second message on the second parchment in such a way that the words and sentences seem to cohere; on the third also you will fit it so that, with the first letters left unharmed, the whole sentence, the number of words, and their size hold together and retain a certain elegance. When these things have been done, place the pattern which you cut out over an equal sheet of parchment, and at the boundaries of the holes mark very fine points as far as the letters which you wish to write extend. Then take a third sheet of parchment and write the letter in continuous words, with a proper order of spaces and size, and also of letters, so that the first sentence and its words are contained within the boundaries of the points. Thus no suspicion of deception then remains: the other person, when he receives it and places the pattern over it, immediately understands the thought of your mind; and for signifying one’s thought to friends in dangerous times, nothing can be found equal to this device, although it is a matter of no slight labor.

*Marcus Tullius.* The meaning is that Cicero devised individual and special characters for individual Latin words, with which, if necessity required, he wrote speech of any length both with very few signs and in the most secret manner. In this way, they say, the speech of Cato, urging that the conspirators be punished, was preserved and committed to writing, when Cicero as consul appointed very rapid writers who could take down spoken words in signs of this sort. The ancients also, in writing out words quickly and [unclear]

[segment 32]
--- Page 094 ---

*Exposition.* 89

briefly and economically, followed a similar method, so that often a single letter signified a word. Valerius Probus, Petrus Crinitus, Angelus Decembrius, and also very ancient monuments most diligently collected by Peter Apian and Bartholomaeus Amantius bear witness to this. But this art of writing will be treated more carefully below.

“Bede, our countryman, in England.” On the computation and speech of the fingers. This invention is found in Bede, *On the Divisions of Times*. When you say one, bending the little finger on the left hand, you will fix its joint in the middle of the palm. When you say two, you will place there the second finger from the little finger, bent. When you say three, you will likewise bend down the third. When you say four, you will raise the little finger again. When you say five, you will likewise set upright the second finger from the little finger. When you say six, you will also raise the third, with only the middle finger, which is called the medical finger, fixed in the middle of the palm. When you say seven, the little finger alone, with the others raised, you will place above the root of the palm. Next to it, when you say eight, place the medical finger. When you say nine, set the shameless finger opposite it. When you say ten, you will fix the nail of the index finger in the middle joint of the thumb. When you say twenty, you will insert the tip of the thumb between the middle joints of the index and shameless fingers. When you say thirty, you will join the nails of the index finger and thumb in a gentle embrace. When you say forty, you will draw the inside of the thumb over the side or back of the index finger, with both alone held upright. When you say fifty, you will bend the thumb, curved like the Greek letter called gamma, toward the palm at its outer joint.

--- Page 095 ---

90 *Exposition.*

When you say sixty, with the thumb curved as above, you will carefully encircle it in front with the index finger bent around it. When you say seventy, you will fill up the index finger, bent around as above, by inserting the thumb, with only its nail held upright across the middle joint of the index finger. When you say eighty, you will fill up the index finger, bent around as above, with the thumb stretched lengthwise, that is, with its nail fixed into the middle joint of the index finger. When you say ninety, you will fix the little nail of the bent index finger into the root of the thumb. Thus far for the left hand. But one hundred you will make on the right hand in the same way as ten on the left; two hundred on the right as twenty on the left; and the rest in the same manner up to nine hundred. Likewise, one thousand on the right as one on the left; two thousand on the right as two on the left; three thousand on the right as three on the left; and the rest up to nine thousand.

Furthermore, when you say ten thousand, you will place the left hand high against the middle of the chest, with the fingers only raised toward the neck. When you say twenty thousand, you will lay the same hand broadly spread upon the chest. When you say thirty thousand, with the same hand facing downward but upright, you will set the thumb into the cartilage at the middle of the chest. When you say fifty thousand, with the same hand facing downward but upright, you will place the thumb on the navel. When you say sixty thousand, with the same hand facing downward, you will grasp the left thigh from above. When you say seventy thousand, you will lay the same hand on the thigh. When you say eighty thousand, you will lay the same hand, facing downward,

--- Page 096 ---

*Exposition.* 91

on the thigh. When you say ninety thousand, with the same hand you will grasp the loins, with the thumb turned toward the groin. But one hundred and two hundred thousand, up to nine hundred thousand, you will complete in the same order on the right side of the body. When you say ten times one hundred thousand, you will interlock both hands with the fingers inserted into one another. And so much concerning numbers expressed by the gesture of the fingers.

To these the same author immediately adds: From the very computation that I have noted above, a certain manual speech can be practiced, both for exercising ingenuity and for playing a game; by it, when the letters have been expressed one by one, someone may hand over words contained in those letters to another who also knows this art, even if he is standing far away, for him to read and understand, either signifying necessary matters more secretly by these means or deceiving the unskilled as though by divination. The order of this game or speech is as follows. When you wish to indicate the first letter of the alphabet, hold one with the hand; for the second, two; for the third, three; and so the rest in order. For example, if you wish to warn a friend placed among ambushers to act cautiously, show three, one, twenty, ten, nine, and five fingers; for the letters of this order signify “act cautiously.”

Thus far the example of dactylology according to Bede, which is varied in many other ways. For if, as was also said before, the sign of unity is set to signify the letter a, and so on in order, let one be assigned on the left hand to the thumb, two to the index finger, three to the middle finger, four to the ring finger, or the third from the thumb, five to the little finger.

[segment 33]
--- Page 097 ---

92 EXPOSITION.

let them be placed on the little finger; likewise, on the right hand let two be understood on the thumb, three on the index finger, four on the middle finger, five on the third finger from the thumb, and six on the little finger. With these things so established, let us observe which numbers arise from the addition, multiplication, or division of which numbers among themselves. For example, twelve arises from the addition of one to eleven, of two to ten, of three to nine, of four to eight, of six to six, and of seven to five. In multiplication, twelve is produced from the drawing of four into three, or of two into six. In division we have twelve if we divide twenty-four by two.

Moreover, when the fingers are only raised, this signifies addition; but when we touch one finger with another, multiplication. But if we bend one and raise the other, division must be understood. Therefore, if one wishes to show the letter m, which occupies the twelfth place, I indicate the number twelve either by addition, or by multiplication, or by division. First, by raising the two little fingers of both hands together with the thumb of the left hand: for the thumb signifies one, while one of the other fingers designates five, the other six; and that number shows the letter m. By multiplication in this way: let us touch the third finger from the thumb of the left hand with the index finger of the right, since three multiplied by four makes twelve; or let us touch the little finger of the right hand with the index finger of the left, which is the same thing, for twice six gives twelve.

--- Page 098 ---

EXPOSITION.
93

It could also be done in another way, namely by the contact of the index finger of the right hand and the middle finger of the left, which show six. These again, by the contact of both thumbs, or of the index finger of the right hand and the thumb of the left, signify the multiplication of the former number by two; but twice six makes twelve. By division thus: first we shall indicate twenty-four, the division of which number by two yields twelve. The gesture of which I spoke above represents division by two, namely if the two thumbs are shown, one raised and the other bent; likewise the thumb and little finger of the left hand, and the index finger of the right bent. For they signify the partition of six by three, that is, two; when twenty-four is divided by this, they show twelve.

So much on dactylogy, or the speech of the fingers, which can be handled otherwise in infinitely many ways, by assigning to fixed positions vowels, consonants, semivowels, double letters, mutes, and liquids, according as it has seemed good to each person to treat such matters. Although these things may appear ridiculous, nevertheless they have the greatest use if they are transferred by a certain analogy to other things. But there are some matters of which the author makes mention partly in the exposition of this passage and partly at the end; yet since they pertain to the treatment of the Steganographia, let them be deferred for a little while, until that point has been reached.

The first kind, dermaticon.) This stratagem is commonly known even to soldiers. Pliny, unless I am mistaken, gives another one in book 26. “Tithymalus,” he says, “our

[segment 34]
--- Page 099 ---

94 EXPOSITION.

our people call the milky herb; others call it goat-lettuce. They relate that when a body has been written on with its milk and it has dried, if ash is sprinkled over it the letters appear; and in this way adulterers preferred to address their mistresses rather than by little tablets.

The second kind, Hyphasmaticon.) The most learned Theodorus Bibliander has “hyphalmicon,” for this reason, as I think: in concealment of this sort nothing of the letters appears before the paper has been immersed in water. Thus, when we write letters with alum, water poured over them will make the paper darker at the letters, which otherwise could not have been read.

Letters are also written in various ways.) Herodotus, book 7, near the end. After Xerxes had resolved to undertake an expedition against Greece, Demaratus, who was at Susa, when he had learned this, judged that it ought to be reported to the Greeks. But since he could not do so otherwise, because it was dangerous lest he be detected, he devised this stratagem: taking the folding tablets of two writing tablets, he scraped the wax from them. Then on their wood he engraved the king’s plan. Once it had been engraved in letters, he again covered it with wax, so that the tablets, to whomsoever they were carried, would cause no trouble among the guards of the roads. When they had been brought to Lacedaemon, the Lacedaemonians could not interpret them before, as I understand it, Gorgo, daughter of Cleomenes and wife of Leonidas, warned them and interpreted the matter herself, ordering that the wax be broken off, for they would find letters on the wood. Listening to her, the Lacedaemonians found the letters, read them, and then sent them on to the Greeks.

--- Page 100 ---

EXPOSITION. 95

Gellius, book 17, chapter 9: “There is also,” he says, “in the records of Greek affairs a certain deep and unexpected hiding place, devised by barbarian cunning. There was a man named Histiaeus, born in a not insignificant place in the land of Asia. At that time King Darius held Asia under his rule. When this Histiaeus was in Persia with Darius, he wanted by secret writing to report certain hidden matters to a certain Aristagoras. He devised this astonishing concealment of letters: his slave, who for a long time had had diseased eyes, had all the hair shaved from his head, as if for the purpose of treatment, and he marked the smooth head with the forms of letters. With these letters he wrote out what he wished. Afterwards he kept the man at home until his hair had grown back. When this had happened, he ordered him to go to Aristagoras. ‘And when you have come to him,’ he said, ‘tell him that I have ordered him to shave your head, just as I myself lately did.’ The slave came to Aristagoras as he had been ordered and delivered his master’s instruction; and Aristagoras, judging that this had not been ordered in vain, did what had been commanded. Thus the letters were delivered.”

Herodotus writes almost the same things; and in that author, book 1, the following is also read. Since Astyages was harsh toward the Medes, Harpagus, ingratiating himself with each of them individually, persuaded the leading men that Astyages ought to be removed from the throne and Cyrus chosen. When these things had been done and prepared, Harpagus, finally wishing to reveal his view to Cyrus, who was among the Persians, and unable to do so otherwise, since the roads were guarded, devised the following plan. After gutting a hare, which he had cleverly procured, so that nothing at all was cut away,

--- Page 101 ---

96 EXPOSITION.

he put inside it a little letter in which he had written what he wished. Sewing up its belly again, he entrusted the hare, together with nets, to one of his household hunters, a very trustworthy man, and instructed him orally that, when he gave the hare to Cyrus, he should tell Cyrus to open it with his own hands and to do so without witnesses. Then he sent the man into Persia. When the messenger had carried this out, Cyrus received the hare, opened it, found the little letter that was inside it, and read it. See Justin, book 1.

Historians also report that doves served as messengers in difficult affairs: in the siege of Mutina, Hirtius the consul sent letters tied to their necks and feet to Decimus Brutus, and Decimus Brutus sent them to Hirtius. Food had been set out in certain very high places, and he had trained the doves, which had first been shut up in darkness and afflicted with great hunger, to fly back and forth from there.

Some people, to avoid suspicion, write on paper with ink, as if they wished to communicate something to a friend about household matters; afterwards they write what is secret in the spaces with urine. Others use lemon juice or onion juice; others use crushed sal ammoniac with water poured over it. Letters written in this way remain hidden before they have been brought near the fire.

Some follow the poet’s advice: “Since a woman can conceal tied papers on her breast, and carry charming notes beneath her joined foot, let the guard beware of these things; instead of paper, let her provide her knowing back, and she will carry the words on her own body. A letter made with fresh milk is also safe and deceives the eyes; touch it with charcoal dust, and you will read it. One made with a moist point also deceives...”

[segment 35]
--- Page 102 ---

EXPOSITION. 97

with the point of a dampened flax-stalk; a little tablet will bring forth the hidden marks, etc. There are also other devices: for example, they paint letters on flint with the fat of a goat or ox; when the flint has been set in vinegar for several hours and then taken out again, it shows to the sight what had been written on it. Likewise, they write with egg white and dip it in ink; when this is scraped off, the letters are seen. Finally, in this passage he wraps many things in obscure words not at all received in common usage, so as not to give wicked men an opportunity either to abuse these things or to devise worse ones. He wrote “race,” with the syllables reversed, for “cera,” as “Roma” for “amor,” “nemilua” for “alumine,” “raphocam” for “camphora,” etc. But since such matters, for the public good, must be treated rather sparingly, and do not belong to our purpose, let us omit explaining everything. For it is enough if it is understood what he wished to signify by this third kind of letters.

*There were also, as Bede bears witness.*) The sense is this: the ancients not only wrote or spoke with words, as we do, but also, with a certain deliberate skill, transferred to the signifying of things whatever the world contains and whatever can be grasped by us. Diodorus Siculus, in Book 4, says: “The letters of the Egyptians are very like various animals, the extremities of human beings, and tools, especially those of craftsmen. For their words are not expressed by the composition of syllables or by letters, but by the form of images, with their meaning handed down to human memory by usage.” They write, for instance, a hawk, a crocodile, a serpent, a human eye, hands, a face, and other things of this sort.

--- Page 103 ---

98 EXPOSITION.

The hawk denotes a thing quickly done, since this bird is almost the swiftest of all; this notation is transferred to domestic matters that are done quickly. The crocodile signifies evil. The eye is interpreted as the guardian of justice and the keeper of the whole body. Among the remaining parts of the body, the right hand with fingers spread out designates freedom, but the left hand with fingers clenched designates tenacity and avarice. In the same way, the figure of the other parts of the body and tools denoted some definite thing; and, once these had been learned by human memory through long use and reflection, people recognized at once what they expressed.

Petrus Crinitus also, Book 7, chapter 2, writes that this manner of writing existed among the Egyptians. Concerning the letters of the Egyptians, which are called hieroglyphic, there is mention among ancient authors; and this can still be observed on obelisks, on which those figures and likenesses of various animals, herbs, rivers, and trees are carved, signifying the philosophy of that nation, as Strabo and Pliny, very weighty authorities, attest. For on the obelisks of the kings Sesostris and Sennosornis, which were brought from Egypt into the city, the interpretation of the nature of things was marked by symbols of this sort and by hieroglyphic signs. Thus the philosophy of the Egyptians is believed first to have been illustrated, as is handed down by the same Pliny and by others. Among other figures,

--- Page 104 ---

EXPOSITION. 99

they were accustomed to use scarabs, bees, rivers, oxen, and other things of that kind. Moreover, by the very image of the bee the symbol of a king was signified, because in performing the duties of life he ought to have no less the sting of justice than the sweet clemency of honey. In the vulture they indicated the genius and majesty of nature, because among these birds only females are found, etc. They also painted many other signs in place of words; concerning these see Horapollo on hieroglyphic letters, and Pliny, Book 36, chapter 11, where according to the more correct reading “Sesostris” is found for “Sesotidis,” and “Phaeron” for “Nuncoreus.” Strabo, Book 17, Cornelius Tacitus, Book 15, Caelius Rhodiginus, *Ancient Readings*, Book 29, chapter 26, and Volaterranus, Book 33, also mention letters of this sort, although Volaterranus writes that they have not yet been discovered by anyone.

*There were those who used accidents.*) The meaning is that whatever exists in the whole nature of things, whether perceived by the intellect or by the senses, can be turned to this use: that it may signify in the same way as words do. Hence it is possible to signify secretly to another person the intention of our mind in countless ways, without suspicion and without any human obstruction: through words; through signs that denote words, as through light, colors, shadows, sounds, odors, tastes; through qualities that affect touch; through shapes, sizes, numbers; through the motion or position of any things whatsoever, such as parts of the body; through gestures of the fingers

[segment 36]
--- Page 105 ---

100 EXPOSITIO.

the nods of the eyes, etc. All these things, indeed, if they are properly handled and arranged according to letters, syllables, words, and speeches, in a fixed order and with numbers assigned to them, furnish human beings with astonishing capacities, and capacities all the more excellent in proportion as one has become more skilled and more practiced in those disciplines that concern the principles, causes, and accidents of sensible things. Such is catoptrics, which teaches how to present visible signs of things to sight in a moment, through certain images projected far from us outside mirrors into the air, and established for signifying some speech; these are easily understood by one who has knowledge of the signs. But by what method it can come about that all things are transferred to the use of signs is not part of the present undertaking, but belongs to the Steganographia. The very learned man Johannes Trithemius recounts the contents of that work in a certain letter to Bostius in these words, etc.

“I now have in hand a great work which, if it is ever published, which may God forbid, the whole world will marvel at; the title of its first book is Steganographia. There will be four books, and each will be divided into at least one hundred chapters. I began this work at the instance of a certain great prince, whom there is no need to name. You ask what this work teaches? It teaches very great things, astonishing and incredible to all who do not know them, which have never been heard from the beginning of the world. For the first book contains more than one hundred methods of writing secretly whatever you wish, without any suspicion, without transposition of letters,

--- Page 106 ---

EXPOSITIO. 101

without any fear, so that there is no person in the world who by natural skill could know, or even suspect, what is contained in my letters, except one who knows the art from me or from someone whom I have taught. The words are plain and familiar, lacking all suspicion, but no one without the art will ever be able to perceive my intention, however learned he may be; and the thing is truly astonishing. The second book contains many more marvelous things. In this art, namely, I can make the conception of my mind known by fire to one who knows my art at any distance whatsoever, at one hundred miles or more, securely, without words, without writings, without signs, through any messenger. If he is apprehended on the road, even if he is questioned under the harshest tortures, he can reveal nothing about my message, because he knows absolutely nothing about it. Whatever may happen, my message will always remain hidden, nor could all the people of the whole world, if they were gathered together, trace it out by natural power. I can also do this without a messenger whenever I wish. I can also indicate my will to one who knows the art while he is sitting in prison, even though I am far absent, however closely he is guarded, even if he were sitting three miles underground. And I can do all these things most broadly and universally, whenever and as often as I wish, naturally, without any superstition or the aid of any spirits whatsoever. These things are marvelous, I admit; but hear things still more marvelous. The third book teaches an art by

--- Page 107 ---

102 EXPOSITIO.

which I can teach an uneducated man, knowing only his mother tongue and never having known a word of the Latin language, in two hours, to write, read, and understand Latin, sufficiently ornately and eloquently, as much as he wishes, so that whoever sees his letters will praise the words and understand that they have been composed in Latin. The fourth book contains many astonishing experiments, but purely natural ones. Namely, I can indicate my mind to one who knows my art while eating or sitting with others, without words and without nods, as extensively as I wish, even while speaking, preaching, playing the organ, or singing, without hindrance to any other action whatever; so that while preaching good things to one who knows, with eyes closed, and many other secrets the fourth book will contain, which are not to be brought before the public. All who hear these things marvel at them; among them are many noble and very learned men, who think that these things are either impossible or altogether supernatural. To these men, and to you, I answer what I have answered to many: that many things are possible naturally which, to those ignorant of the powers of nature, seem impossible or even supernatural. And behold, I speak before my God, who knows all things, that those marvelous things which I have spoken of, however much you may be able to write or believe, are nevertheless all purely natural, without any deception, without superstition, without magical art, without

[segment 37]
--- Page 108 ---

EXPOSITION. 103

without invocation or the ministry of any spirits whatsoever. I have said this so that, if perhaps at some time a rumor should reach you that I either know or can do marvelous things, you would not, I say, think me a magician, but a philosopher. For what happened to Albert the Great, that most profound investigator of natural things, namely that because of the marvelous things he accomplished by the hidden power of nature he was considered a magician by the common people, I am certain could happen similarly to me. If you ask whence I have these things, which no one else knows, listen. I learned them not from a man nor through a man, but by the revelation of I know not whom. For in the present year, on a certain occasion, when I was thinking what new thing I might discover that would be hidden from everyone, I began to consider whether I could devise those marvelous things that I have mentioned. After long imagining, when I had entirely despaired of it as an impossible matter, I went to sleep, and during the night I lay at rest, laughing to myself at my folly, because I had attempted to seek things so impossible. That same night someone stood by me, saying: “The things you have thought, O Trithemius, are not vain, although they are impossible for you, and neither you nor anyone with you will be able to discover them.” And I said to him: “If, then, they are possible, tell me, I beg you, how they may be done.” And opening his mouth, he taught me about each point in order, showing how the things I had vainly pondered for many days could easily be done. Behold, before God, I do not lie but speak the truth: I have so far taught these things to no one, although many, promising great rewards, have often asked me, except one prince to whom I am writing, whom I have made more certain, by clear proof, of the possibility of the art.

--- Page 109 ---

104 EXPOSITION.

Nor is it fitting that others know these things except princes; otherwise many evils would be done through them: betrayals, deceptions, fornications, and whatever else evil men might wish. But those who use the art well could accomplish much good for the commonwealth through it. Moreover, I can teach all these things in every language of the whole world, even one I have never heard. These things, Bostius, I wished to tell you so that you would not believe me idle.

Thus far the words of Trithemius. Finally, there are other very great uses of these books of the Polygraphy, which we shall discuss in our own books On the Method of Teaching and Learning. For whatever these things may seem to be, they contain more in reserve than they promise on the surface. On Steganography, whose principles lie hidden and concealed in these books, God willing, we shall treat separately.

THE FIRST BOOK
of the Polygraphy.

There are twenty-four letters among the signs of the Latin language. When these are placed in order before the same number of significant words, they serve most abundantly in every respect, according to the wish of each person working in this art, for secrets. Now these paraphrastic sets are artificially arranged in such a way that they produce an entirely Latin discourse, if only one word is taken from each order. And it should be noted that, by reason of our German speech, the twenty-fourth letter, w, is assumed at the end of the alphabet, since all the remaining letters perform the service of both languages.

--- Page 110 ---

EXPOSITION.

We have placed the designating words in order with such skill that, whichever one you take from any single line, once the whole deduction has been made, agreement will remain. Care is needed, however, so that from any line of the syntax you take only one, and so that no monad does injury to the binary. It must also be provided that, whenever you approach the cabalistic operation, you first count each minute of the night, and join them in due proportion to the degrees of the following day. But if the minutes, by the secret arrangement, do not suffice all the way to the degrees of the day, then the windy lot must be brought back, with counsel, to the prudent arch-measurer. Nor is it an obstacle if by chance the night falls back upon the head of the day, since the prudent practitioner in the art can depart from the front by a voluntary addition by as much as he sees the night exceed the light of day. And this is to be understood throughout the whole work of the Polygraphy concerning the individual chapters. Yet we call that consideration unity, by which the binary is made certain by the known little sign of reversal, lest it happen to go astray. From the monad, by the established rule, the binary will stand, and it is made a monad. Nothing is placed here in vain; nothing is adduced without cause or idly.

[segment 38]
--- Page 111 ---
Therefore we must warn, as necessary, that anyone who wishes to copy this work of our Polygraphia, either in whole or in part, should first take diligent care not to depart in any respect from our archetype, nor skip, neglect, or transpose any word whatever. For truly, if he omits a single letter or word in essential matters, if he changes a word contrary to its nature, he has made the whole procedure of the work useless. Therefore let him either copy it properly, or not copy it.

There follow the words of the first order, with minutes, corresponding to the degrees.
