The End

--- Page 304 ---

*BOOK I.*

Let us rejoice that the end of the first book of our *Polygraphy* is at hand; made readier by it for what remains, we judge that we must attempt what the powers of our talent may be. Newly discovered things sometimes trouble the beginner, because one whom all will follow has no one preceding him. The first beginning of any invention is harder; afterward, once it has been made firm, it gives the author greater confidence.

And so, completing the first book of our invention with God's favor and aid, we have divided the whole into three hundred eighty-four small units, assigning to each, in order, its proper grades of paraphrases, as may be seen. And these form a sufficiently beautiful Christian harmony, with sixteen varied stations, joined throughout in due order by more than two hundred nine thousand phrases. Nothing is lacking here for students of these inventions of ours, so that they may have the fullest knowledge and capacity for announcing mystical, secret, and hidden matters, even very far off, with the greatest safety.

All things contained in the circle of the art proceed from a point; their atoms make up a circular line, and, by the multitude of light, a straight one. Whatever

--- Page 305 ---

*OF THE POLYGRAPHY*

is outside the line serves the common reckoning of day, not night. For night, always enclosed within the bounds of small units and paraphrases, never goes beyond them. Therefore, whenever you have crossed the straight line of day and have not fulfilled the purpose of the nocturnal syntax, turn the head back to the tail, and, with a binding made, let there be a fastening of both to one another.

Arrange the shadow in such a way that it does not confuse the sun by likeness of sound. Do not fear embarrassment if, in the first calculation, the day has not included the shadow; rather, count up the small units of the lines until the necessary proportion follows according to your wish. You will also be able to make a leap from the first into the second, knowing that in the first creation of days evening and morning were made one day. Therefore, when evening has been completed, lead the day back into the following one, and, once the sign has been given through the point, fix the known mystery for the binary and the monad together.

But in order that you may be able to avoid this difficulty, divide the great revolutions of our whole circle by arithmetical degrees; and where day is repeated in place of night, set the mark of Archimedes upon the flying one, lest the immature ternary return to unity bina-

[segment 69]
--- Page 306 ---

*Book I.* 301

... block by means of the binary. For this reason we have placed the minutiae before the individual degrees by an artful invention, so that we might relieve the person working in them from the labor of numbering the paraphrases. If you are industrious, diligent, and studious in the matters that we have handed down as the principles of the new art, believe me, you will find greater things hidden there than we have promised. Indeed, since many natural secrets are contained in these things, you should know one thing, and not the least: that by the prudent operation of metathesis, whenever you wish, you will make even someone highly skilled in this art ignorant, someone seeing blind, and someone learned unskilled. If the false opinion of the Hebrews were true, that there are eighteen thousand worlds for every human being, this invention of ours would provide each person with individual ways of safely and secretly writing and communicating, very widely, whatever secrets they wished in Latin.

JOAN-

--- Page 307 ---

302

OF JOHANNES TRI-
THEMIUS, ABBOT
OF PEAPOLIS, OF THE OR-
DER OF SAINT BENEDICT,
*Polygraphia*, Book Two, beginning
of the chapter.

The reckoning of mortal beings, by art, attempts to imitate nature, which admits no calculation of the monad. Therefore, although the arrangement of the preceding book of the *Polygraphia*, through the extension and metathesis of particulars, is in a sense sufficient for the universals of this art in the infinite progressions of number, nevertheless a definite consideration has moved us to join one thing to the other. The condition of light is different in the two cases, but night preserves the order of its course inviolate in both. A prudent reader, from these small inventions of ours, not polished with complete exactness, will easily be able to discover greater and more polished ones, if he is vigorous in outstanding talent. Whatever visible thing is placed in the whole circle of the world readily supplies support for the service of this new art of ours. Air and fire, water and

--- Page 308 ---

*Polygraphia*, Book II. 303

earth, springs and rivers, trees and fruits, flowers and grasses, words and names, accident and substance, stones and sand, dung and excrement, things and terms, honorable and shameful things, woods and metals, hard and liquid things, existing and non-existing things, history and fable, beasts and brute animals, human beings and beasts of burden, each thing and all things, which either speech can express or the mind can think, provide the broadest field for our *Polygraphia*, and promise that the art will be enlarged to infinity, as far as mortals can establish it. For just as, from unity through the binary, the ternary generates the perfect number in the quaternary, so the completion of this invention, by establishing its root through the quaternary in the twice-perfect binary, gives us an infinite number under the minutiae of things and the paraphrases of the *Polygraphia*. For even if among us all our things are finite, nevertheless many things often occur which seem infinite to us because of the slowness of our understanding. Who will be able to count the drops of the sea? And who will penetrate the inscrutable heart of man? Just as a finite and circumscribable number can be led in progression to infinity, so the instruction of this art is enlarged beyond measure by varied metathesis. Whatever is seen anywhere in the whole world, for our instruc-

--- Page 309 ---

304 *Polygraphia*

tion conforms in every respect, nor is there anything known to us that is free of mystery, if the method is understood by the master. We, however, persuaded by reason, remain under the service of the binary and briefly explain the smallest part of the whole. Nor, that we might raise our undertaking to weightier matters, is an occasion given; if it is offered to us in an acceptable way, we shall not refuse to make clearer things manifest to those who desire them.

For it is written: Where there is no hearing, never pour out speech. Therefore, adding the second book of the *Polygraphia* to the first, we keep its same form and method, placing 308 paraphrases in the light of day by simple progression, in honorable memory of the most chaste Mother of God. If the time had been suitable, ability could have provided more graceful things, both to add brilliance to the material and to give sufficient advancement to the form. The first inventor of any art transmits greater things to posterity, because, since the beginnings of an art are hard and unfamiliar, no subtle invention is found completed from its origin. With the help

--- Page 464 ---
459
With the help of our Lord Jesus Christ, Savior of faithful souls, who lives in the sacred triune unity with the Father and the Holy Spirit, threefold in persons and one God in substance, incomprehensible, almighty, and everlasting, from whom, in whom, and through whom every good, beautiful, and true thing proceeds, we have brought the second book of this Polygraphia to its end. To him be praise, honor, power, and glory, who through the innocence of his death freed our race from the ruin of eternal perdition, and by perpetual mercy reopened the entrance to eternal life for all who believe in him with a pure conscience. Line by line we have set down three hundred and eight paraphrases, deliberately avoiding a more prolix rambling digression. For at the beginning of this night-work it seemed enough to us if we had given those who desire greater things a reason for seeking them out, since both time and method were lacking to us for them. Farewell, friendly reader. From Peapoli, on the next-to-last day of the month of February, in the year of the Lord 1508.
IOAN-

[segment 95]
--- Page 465 ---

Book III of the Polygraphia of Johannes Trithemius, abbot of Peapol, of the Order of Saint Benedict, begins.

What, from the beginning of human creation, confounded the unity of language? Was it not the division of bodies and the diversity of minds? For as Truth testifies in the Gospel: “Out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks.” Language is the instrument for bringing forth those conceptions which the mind has thought within itself, in its inward parts. Yet there are several modes of this ministry, by which the secrets of the mind are brought to sense; their properties vary both by usage and by art. Usage, introduced at pleasure, is common to many; art, however, to few. Finally, how much the very unregulated usage of speech has varied among the Germans alone in six hundred years, no one will readily judge who has not often reread Otfrid, the monk of Wissembourg, in his poems in the German language. The sea flows and ebbs; human beings, once they have died, do not return again to the present life. Customs succeed

--- Page 466 ---

customs, and the vigor of mortal minds is always declining where better things are concerned. Who today, among the Germans, those ancient emulators of the Greeks, retains knowledge of the old idiom? In the chronicles and old histories of the Franks, Hunibald, Doracus, and the other writers who preceded us by a thousand years mixed many things said in ancient verse and in the prose of the German tongue into their compositions; these are understood today by few. Why do I speak of ancient times, when our own native idiom among the East Franks, Swabians, Bavarians, Rhinelanders, Moselle people, Cologne people, Saxons, and Thuringians undergoes notable variation every day? Human beings change in time; customs also vary; minds change; clothing also varies. The simplicity of languages is altered, the old property of idioms is varied, and nothing stable is found in human affairs. For there is a vicissitude of all things, and variety succeeds the simplicity of minds. Who will be able to bring this perpetual flowing away, so to speak, back to the concord of unity? For many times pass away, as Scripture says, and knowledge will be manifold. That we add a third book to the two preceding books of the Polygraphia has not lacked a special reason. For since human wills are varied, it is rightly the case that manifold methods are given for honorable operations.

--- Page 467 ---

In those, day illuminates night; in these, however, day is without light. There, light leads shadow around in light; here, however, the blind illuminates the blind, and, existing and remaining without eyes, supplies in darkness the sight which it does not have. Here night clouds day over with perpetual darkness, and one walking in darkness provides guidance without light, under a blind leadership. These paraphrases serve all people throughout the world, and in every language they transmit any secret whatever safely, securely, and without danger to the cabalistic binary. Latin raises laughter among barbarians, and barbarism itself creates suspicion among Latin readers. Yet it matters nothing, or little, since whatever the suspicion may be, it will nevertheless contain every most secret mystery. Let the diligent operator recall to mind the rules and precepts that we have handed down, lest he labor uselessly and in vain for himself and for others. Understanding is needed, for without it the usefulness of no art will be established. We have shown in a few words a path to the greatest things, and have given by a finite mode a way to infinite things. From the things given and received, greater things are easily prepared for friends. Reader, remember that the retrograde mark can be fixed in every place until you take hold of the mark of rest. Farewell. From Peapol, on the first day of March, in the year of the Lord 1508.

MAOI

[segment 104]
--- Page 512 ---

What we promised at the beginning of the third book of our work Polygraphy, to provide the shadow and likeness of a new gloss before the inexperienced, we have sufficiently fulfilled by way of example, with economy of labor. We have entrusted the night, safely serving secrets, to be guided by the shadows of periphrases, and we have inserted the numbers in the order of four lines, all the way to the end, adapted for the use of those who wish it. The places are fixed degrees of minutiae, numbering three thousand one hundred fifty, although, if you wish, the moments of the hours remain infinite, for concealing whatever secret may arise in every nation and language of the world. The orb, coming into being linearly from a point, as often as night exceeds the image of light, you will be able, as much as you wish and as need requires, to bring back to the path by which you came. If much of the night remains, bring the dragon’s tail back to the original caudal place at the head. But let the binary be altogether certified by the marks and signs of unity. He who has been very hungry should labor much, so that he may have something to eat. Nor is it a matter of great labor to lead wherever you wish something that proceeds of its own accord. Here, therefore, we have set the end to the third book of our Polygraphy, in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit. Gentle reader, farewell.

From Peapoli, on the 7th day of the month of March, in the year of our Lord Jesus Christ, our Savior, 1508, in the eleventh indiction.

IOAN-

--- Page 513 ---

508

The Fourth Book of the Polygraphy of Johannes Trithemius, abbot of the monastery of St. James the Greater, Apostle, at Wuerzburg, formerly of Sponheim, of the Order of Saint Benedict, begins.

The order of reasoning required that our fourth book of Polygraphy be made unlike the preceding books in character, invention, and ease, so that those who wish to share in the secrets of our matters may attain this not by one method but by various and diverse ones. We have extended a magpie comprehensible by a very easy invention, so that night, always joined through a minute point to the monad, may be recognized in the light of day in any order. Here no disagreement is found between unity and the binary; nor will the person wishing to perform the art need to have the instrument of the art at hand, since the thing following the first, without an intermediary, reveals the hidden mystery.

--- Page 514 ---

From this, as a prudent, understanding, and experienced person in the science, he will satisfy the operation at every time and place. The binary receives from unity, whence it produces the ternary. The knowledge of this cryptology is easy at first sight, once the moon has been illuminated by the sun even at a single point. Although it can also happen that a knowing person becomes skilled without the guidance of a master. Yet I think this is to be understood only in this book, and in none of the preceding ones. As for the following books, judgment will be given in its proper place. Among all the cabalistic methods of secrets that we have given up to the present, the procedure of this fourth book can most easily be extended to infinity. Therefore it is enough that I have handed down to those who desire fuller things both the form and the reason for the example. The light of understanding easily penetrates all these things.

I wrote at Peapoli, on the ninth day of March, in the year of the Lord one thousand five hundred eight.

A Baldach

--- Page 555 ---

Having shown the possibility of our new invention, briefly and yet sufficiently, I do not think that I myself should be wearied any longer on this same occasion, since the method provides a path without limit, if anyone should need an example for further things. Nor do I think it at all necessary to recall to memory the things that have been recited in the preceding sections, since a shrewd reader, if he is studious, will also be able, with the Lord's help, to discover the greater things that lie hidden in these matters. We have handed over to the wise and studious man both the material and the cause; if he wishes to use these prudently, he will surpass our invention by his own talent.

But whoever desires this path, laid out in a fairer shade, should join hand and mind to talent, and should make for himself a route more beautifully adorned by paraphrase. Let anyone whom these things delight keep firmly in memory that he must always remain within the prescribed circle; if he transgresses it in any way, the whole operation vanishes uselessly. There are many names of human beings; indeed there are almost infinite words for things, villages, cities, provinces, herbs, trees, stones, and verbs, all of which serve our instructions. From these it is established that it is not impossible for a book and a method to be made, if diligence and talent are at hand, which may surpass our inventions without limit. But the man who is of such great dullness that he cannot understand these childish matters of ours: when will he penetrate the subtler things that remain?

We set an end to the fourth book of the Polygraphia, to which we join the fifth in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ.

IOAN-

--- Page 556 ---
551

HERE BEGINS THE FIFTH BOOK OF THE POLYGRAPHIA OF JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS, ABBOT OF SAINT JAMES THE APOSTLE AT WÜRZBURG.

The things which, with the help of our Lord Jesus Christ, we have set forth as best we could in the four preceding books for the completion of our Polygraphia, reasonably require that in the fifth book we briefly treat the remaining precepts and methods of this instruction that are still left. Therefore in it we shall give a sufficiently concise method by metathesis, that is, by transposition of letters, by which a friend may write to a friend whatever he wishes securely, safely, and most secretly. We read that Caesar Augustus used this manner of writing in secret matters. Although this method of sending messages is safe in many ways, nevertheless

--- Page 557 ---
552 POLYGRAPHIAE

nevertheless to those who look at it it is regarded as suspect; for as soon as the transposition of letters is seen, anyone who is curious immediately suspects that something secret lies hidden underneath. Now the modes of this invention are almost infinite; from them, for the sake of a necessary introduction, we have marked out the following ones for the reader, reserving the rest to be investigated by his own talent.

First, therefore, we have placed before him the canonical table of this practice, and have set beneath it a second, reversed one. In these two tables the root and foundation of the transposition just mentioned are placed altogether, and on this the structure of the whole building is set. For when these two tables have been duly known and understood, no transposition of letters will be able to escape the diligent worker. To these we have joined twenty-four alphabets in order, so that we might provide the reader with an abundance for transposing letters. The use of these consists as desired in individual ones, or, if you prefer, also in all of them. If you have secret communication with many people, distribute individual alphabets to your individual associates; but commit to memory what M

[segment 112]
--- Page 558 ---

BOOK V.

what you have given to each person. To the first, then, let the first alphabet be entrusted; to the second, the second; to the third, the third; and so let it be done successively with the rest. But if, because of the number of affairs or of one's household, the alphabets we have marked out should not suffice for everything, or if some of them should seem too open and obvious, we can introduce various new transpositions, whose number may be very great, while the method of the secret always remains perpetually hidden. Let us give an example of this: let b stand for a, f for b, l for c, p for d, t for e, z for f, c for g, m for h, and so on successively through the whole usual alphabet, so that between any assumed letter there lie three unused and transposed letters. Afterward four, then five, more or fewer, yet in such a way that the whole alphabet, as though turned back into a circle, comes together, and no letter at all is omitted, but each one is placed in the same sequence for another in its own order. In whatever way you have written it, you must either know it firmly by heart or entrust it firmly to memory, lest through forgetfulness

--- Page 559 ---

you fall into error and can no longer read what you yourself had written. For the transposition of letters can be varied in as many ways as, according to the estimation of the human intellect, which, enclosed in temporal operation within a mortal body, does not properly grasp infinities, there will arise a number innumerable to a mortal man. Hence anyone who wishes to have more methods of writing by metathesis, and methods removed from our instruction, if he is even a little ingenious and diligent, will easily attain the desired effect in his operation. For the human mind can accomplish very great things if it is aided by study and art. But he who despises the smallest things will never reach the greater ones. I know that there will be very many who will mock these studies of ours as empty and childish; to them we give this answer: that we shall be entirely content, provided that these things please those at whose request they have been composed by us.

The direct table follows.

In this reversed table, by transposition of the letters, you likewise have as many alphabets as we set down in the preceding regular table. For, as you see, under metathesis there are twenty-four toward the right; likewise the same number toward the left, twenty-four downward, and twenty-four upward. These, multiplied through ninety-six twenty-four times and reduced in the manner of an egg to four sides, make no fewer than 112 alphabets.

The same expanded table follows.

This is the first figure of the expansion of the regular table, containing five alphabets by metathesis, with the letters transposed as is seen. For in the first alphabet b is taken for a, c is taken for b, and so on in sequence, as is clearer to one who looks at it. In the second alphabet c is taken for a; in the third d, in the fourth e, in the fifth f, as the arrangement of the order shows.

JOHAN-

--- Page 577 ---
572

JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS OF SPANHEIM TO THE READER.

Since all the letters of the Greeks and Hebrews are numerals, I have often wondered that the ingenious Latinity of the ancients did not also make its own letters serve for numbers. The Hebrews, by doubling certain letters, established twenty-eight signs sufficient for every number. The Greeks, by adding four characters, relieved this lack of letters with similar foresight. We therefore, having twenty-four letters, why do we not also make ours numerals, since we can likewise find four duplicated ones? Let us give four as an example, and there will be enough for every number: m and final iij; likewise r and final 2; likewise long s and final or closed s; likewise closed v and open u. When we add these to the twenty-four ordinary letters, we have twenty-eight characters sufficient for all numbers.

--- Page 578 ---
573

And it will be no matter of great labor for those who already know letters to grasp this method of numbering, if they once commit it to memory. For this invention of ours will be able to be useful to many, since it comprises many thousands in number with few letters, all of which can be discerned and understood at first sight, more clearly than light, without any calculation. Many, less experienced in the art of calculation, think that this cannot be done so easily by the figures of algorism, especially in large numbers. For as soon as you inspect with your eye a number, however great, you will be able both to understand it fully and to express it, without circumlocution or division of speech. To show how easy this is, it is fitting that we set down the letters themselves reduced to number.

I wrote this on 16 March, in the year 1508.

Order

So now, reader, you have the method by which every writable number can sufficiently be set down and written with few letters, whose extension, as you see, proceeds to infinity. But, as it was once commonly said in a proverb, “The tired ox plants his foot firmly”: whoever wishes to be content with the old ways should follow the ancient method of numbering. So that the unlearned may understand it more easily, it will not be tedious for me to give, in what follows, the form used by Hunibald, the historian of the Franks, and also by Bede, Rabanus, the monk Helperic, and many of the ancients in chronicles and ecclesiastical calculations, as follows.

The method of numbering of Hunibald the Frank and others.

Up to this point we have set out, by way of example, the method of the ancient Franks of Germany for writing numbers with Latin letters, so that we might also provide knowledge of it to those who do not know it. But the fact that, in setting down the figures of the algorism, we departed from the common rule of computation was done not from error but for a reasonable cause, namely so that we should not be burdened by excessive progression. There were also some who wrote larger numbers in this manner:

cm. 100000    dcccM. 800000    lxcM.
ccm. 200000   xcM. 900000      LxxxM.
cccm. 300000  xxM. 1000000     lxxxxM.
dcm. 400000   xxxM. 2000000    ixCm.
dm. 500000    xxxxM. 3000000   MCM.
dcM. 600000   lxM. 4000000     xCCM.
DCCM. 700000  CM. 5000000      CCCM.

--- Page 583 ---
578
The sixth book of the Polygraphia of Johannes Trithemius, abbot of Saint James the Greater the Apostle at Würzburg, begins.

Before any invention and use of letters, in the beginning of the world, when human beings were still crude in understanding and uncultivated in manners, under the government of the shared succession of three intelligences, as some suppose, succeeding one another in order through one thousand sixty-three years, the influences of the planets were in the sphere, presiding in their own way over bodies on earth. The ancient speculators on this matter say that Saturn, from the beginning of the world, chiefly presided by influence over things germinating and being born on earth, with his intelligence governing him, whom they called Orisiel, that is, the life-giver of God, for three hundred fifty-four lunar years, likewise four months, and as many days and hours. We recount these things as poetic, not as things we confirm. After Saturn, Venus is said to have assumed dominion; after Venus, Jupiter. But after Jupiter, Mercury, the fourth ruler in order, adorned the minds of human beings

--- Page 584 ---
with various arts. Under the three intelligences just mentioned, that is, while Saturn, Venus, and Jupiter were ruling, they assert that human beings did not have the use or knowledge of letters. Therefore, whenever one person wanted to announce some secret to another, he did this not in writings, since the service of letters was lacking, but symbolically, by certain likenesses of animals, trees and plants, or of some other sensible things, according as each had agreed among themselves.

The time of Saturn, as they say, gave to human beings manners properly suited to his influence. For it made them farmers, hunters, founders of cities, crude, woodland-dwelling, gloomy, simple, uncultivated in life and manners, and living a long time in the flesh. After Orisiel, the intelligence of Saturn, as those who hold this opinion say, Anael, the mover of Venus, received the government of the world by influence and continued it for three hundred fifty-four years, and for four months, days, and hours. Under him human beings began to be more cultivated, to build houses and cities more frequently, and to invent various manual arts, namely weaving, wool-working, and many other things similar to these. They also began to sink more deeply into the pleasures of the flesh, to take beautiful wives, and, after many children had been begotten from them, they fell notably away from natural simplicity. They invented games and songs, began to play the cithara, and, following the movement of the flesh, more eagerly devised whatever they affirm belongs to the influence of Venus. The third intelligence of Jupiter

[segment 118]
--- Page 585 ---
They named the third intelligence of Jupiter Zachariel. Taking up dominion over the world after Anael, it made human beings restless with desire for rule, to conduct hunts, make tents, and adorn themselves with various garments. While he ruled, in accordance with the character of Jupiter, who deprived his father of his kingdom, a great division arose between the good and the wicked: the former calling upon God, the latter devoted to the flesh and the world. Under this rule Adam, the first man, died, leaving to all posterity the testament of dying once.

The fourth mover, or angel of Mercury, Raphael, exercising influence after Zachariel upon worldly things, began in the 1055th year of the world, at the end of the month of February, and lasted until the 1410th year of the world. Under him the use of letters is said first to have been invented. The letters at that time, however, as they report, were very different from those which the world has today, devised in the form and likeness of trees, plants, and animals. Yet afterward, with time, they were altered in many ways, and took on an easier manner of formation and a more cultivated adornment. For the fictions of the poets have said that Mercury is the patron of all literature, promising that great secrets of things lie hidden beneath fables. After this, when the human race had multiplied and had spread everywhere over the whole earth, the unity of letters did not long remain; rather, the separation of bodies and minds far from one another discovered diverse and various modes of writing, concerning which the account would be long for us. Plato the philosopher

--- Page 586 ---
in the Phaedrus relates that a certain philosopher named Theuth first invented letters and the use of writing, together with the system of weights, numbers, and the game of dice, and taught them to Thamus, king of the Egyptians. But concerning the other ancient men who are said to have handed down special letters to almost individual nations and peoples, which remain in their use down to the present day, it is not my purpose on this occasion to set out or describe the account. For the more learned among our people know that Cadmus, son of Agenor, king of the Phoenicians, is said first to have brought sixteen letters into Greece: α, ε, γ, δ, ζ, ω, λ, μ, ν, ι, θ, ξ, π, σ, τ, ψ. To these Palamedes, at the time of the Trojan War, is said to have added four: ξ, θ, φ, χ. And Pythagoras later added the letter υ, so that there would be twenty-one; ζ, κ, and ω were subsequently invented by others. Gordanis, bishop of the Goths, gave his people letters proper to them.

When the unity of language had been confounded, the simplicity of characters also was varied in many ways. From this it came about that the use of letters, in the course of time, claimed such license for itself that not only individual peoples, but even individual persons, fashioning new characters for letters at their own discretion, prepared hiding places for their mysteries. Yet every writing in an unusual character will not be free from the suspicion of those who see it. Nevertheless, many kings, tyrants, and princes of old devised for themselves a private manner of writing, separate from common use, caring little for the suspicion of others,

--- Page 587 ---
provided that what they wished to remain secret was not perceived. Moved by imitation of these, and wishing to bring this work of Polygraphy, as far as possible, to its destined completion, in this sixth book, which is also the last, I have resolved to call back from the lower world certain characters of the ancients which have long since passed out of human use, lest they perish entirely. First, then, we shall set forth the ancient manner of writing of the Northmen.

ON THE ALPHABET
AND MARVELOUS MANNER OF WRITING
of the Northmen, at the time when
they were ravaging.

All the letters of the Greeks and Palestinians are numerical, as has been said before, whereas of the Latin letters, which we Germans also use, only seven are referred to numbers, namely c, d, i, l, m, y, and x. The Northmen, however, with the Asiatic Dacians from the Scandian islands, which are now called Gothic, having once gone out, roamed through Gaul for more than forty years and devastated it most grievously. In order to look after their secret affairs, as Bede the English monk bears witness, they invented a new and beautiful manner of writing from the letters of the Greeks, as follows. For they adapted the numbers of the Greeks, contained in ten of the twenty-four letters, to Latin characters, and under the scheme

[segment 119]
--- Page 588 ---

under a foreign scheme they prudently concealed our letters: that is, in the same way in which we use numbers in place of letters, when, for example, we put 1 for a, 2 for b, 3 for c, 4 for d, and so on in sequence to the end of the alphabet. A fine invention indeed, and sufficiently secure, which neither a Greek nor a Latin will easily understand. For the whole alphabet is not found in it, but only nine letters and one numerical sign, namely g, which among them signifies six; and in this mode of writing it signifies f, the sixth letter of the Latins. Furthermore, yielding to our German language, in which we were born, we have placed double w throughout as the twenty-fourth letter of our nation, which, according to the Greek number, we have formed thus: [unclear]. Now let us set down this writing of the Northmen in imitation of them, from the following order of Greek letters.

[unclear alphabet table]

--- Page 589 ---

By the service of this alphabet the Northmen wrote by secure transmission whatever they wished to keep hidden among themselves. No Greek could understand this manner of writing, since, although all these characters are Greek, nevertheless, because they represent Latin letters according to numerical order, they could be read neither by a Greek nor by a Latin who had not previously attained knowledge of this device. This mode of writing can also be varied in many ways, when the numbers and the letters are changed by transposition. And this can be done with the customary Greek letters, according to the wish and judgment of each industrious writer. Nor will the manner of forming these characters be very difficult, especially for one who has had a little practice. The characters described above are also sufficiently suited to the German language; with them we can denote everything that comes before us, whatever language we may know. Whenever iota is set by itself, so that it may be known to denote the letter k, the tenth letter of the alphabet, it is marked above.

ON THE ALPHABET
AND METHOD OF WRITING
OF THE ANCIENT FRANKS.

Many hold the opinion that the Germans once had letters from the Greeks, and they steadfastly affirm that they also used the Greek language, especially in their sacred rites. They say

--- Page 590 ---

that there is a great agreement between the Greek language and our own, although the long interval of time has wholly extinguished that correspondence. That most learned man, once my teacher, Johannes Camerarius Dalburgius, most reverend bishop of the church of Worms, had collected some thousands of Greek words which signify the same thing in both languages, Greek and German. And Caesar testifies in the Commentaries that the ancient Druids, priests of the sacred rites in Gaul, used the Greek language, especially in sacred matters. But when the Romans were laying waste the world, they at last violently imposed the use of their own letters, together with their dominion, upon many nations. Finally, the remnants of the Trojans, who were afterward called Franks, in the four hundred and fortieth year before the incarnation of the Lord, left the borders of the Scythians under the leadership of King Marcomer, came into Germany, and received settlements from their brothers the Saxons at the mouths of the Rhine. Thereafter, for nine hundred and fifty years and more, waging almost continual war with the Romans and Gauls, as Hunibald is the authority, they at last subjected nearly all Europe to their empire or kingdom. Yet they did not abolish the letters of the Romans once they had accepted them; rather, mingled among Germans and Gauls, they learned their language and preserved their letters and their form of writing. Wastbald, the writer of the histories of this people, wrote in his native language books on the deeds of the kings, dukes, and

[segment 120]
--- Page 591 ---

586
of princes over nearly seven hundred fifty-eight years, with the characters added below, as Hunibald says, which he mentions in his volume that he published in eighteen books on the origin of the Franks.

Unless a copyist's error has deceived me, I have represented Wasibald's alphabet suitably, as I think. In it some characters differ entirely from Greek letters, while others seem partly to resemble them. Yet I do not know what to judge: whoever their inventor was, although he did not keep the form of the Greek letters, he did observe the order of the letters. The manuscript, decayed by excessive age, could scarcely be read; hence I fear that I have been deceived by some interpolator rather than recovered Hunibald's true and certain meaning. But let us pass on to what remains, since if Hunibald's statement is true, we shall have written well; but if it is someone’s invention,

--- Page 592 ---

587
it will neither harm us nor do injury to the reader. There follows another invented alphabet found in Hunibald's manuscript, to which this heading was attached:

Whoever wishes to use the characters written above should know that they are to be written below the lines, in the manner of the Greeks, not above as the Latins do. Likewise this must be observed: of the double letters, the first forms are written at the beginning and in the middle of words, but the second forms always at the end.

--- Page 593 ---

588
FROM THE GRAMMARS OF OTFRID, MONK OF WEISSENBURG.

We read that Charles, king of the Franks, surnamed the Great, taking pity on the barbarism of his nation, attempted, with Nanno, Theobald, Albinus, and Berenger as helpers, to regulate the German language. And although, as Turpin bears witness, he had begun so arduous a work, he did not complete it, being delayed by frequent incursions of wars, but, overtaken by death, left it unfinished; nor did another arise after him who would recall the care of this matter to mind. But Otfrid, the aforementioned monk, once a hearer and disciple of Rabanus of Fulda, under Louis the Pious, the son of Charles himself, attained that Grammar, although imperfectly, and wrote much in our German language, which seems to sound with the wonderful dignity of regular instruction. For he composed many poems according to the same rule, in both heroic and elegiac meter; moreover he also composed some things in prose for Louis, king of Germany, son of Louis the Pious and brother of the emperor Lothar; also for Otgar, archbishop of Mainz, for the monks of Saint Gall, and for others. Having obtained fragments of this Grammar, I too extracted the present characters of the alphabet from many,

--- Page 594 ---

589
so that they would not perish completely. Moreover, on Otfrid's testimony, it is established that Charles devised several alphabets of his own, which he could use securely throughout his very wide kingdom in secret matters with individual prefects. From these, with Otfrid supplying the material, we commit a few things to these letters.

ZI A P Z T N 7 t
† B L H C O G V X
K S m I U R Q Y
T D F K L ß T ψ z
C o e Λ m ψ s Ψ w

Charles the Great, king and most Christian emperor, waged war with the Saxons for no fewer than thirty years. At last, overcoming them by the sword, he converted them to the Christian faith. But fearing that they might again apostatize from the faith, as they had done many times, he appointed certain secret investigators, to whom he granted judicial authority, who, traveling throughout all Saxony, were to inquire secretly and diligently into the faith and morals of the people; and whomever they found to have apostatized from the faith, or to be robbers, adulterers, blasphemers, despisers of the Church and its priests and commandments, or defilers of the Christian faith by notorious crimes...

[segment 121]
--- Page 595 ---

or recalling or soliciting the people to paganism, they were, without delay, by imperial and royal authority and with impunity, to hang by the noose, or otherwise kill as they could. And so that this institution might remain forever unshaken, he gave the same men power to substitute others also, suitable under certain conditions, who, enjoying the aforesaid faculty, should exercise with impunity the office of inquiry and of death against the guilty. Finally he prescribed for them secret laws, hidden signs, and also a form of oath, by which they might proceed justly in judging and punishing, be known mutually to one another while remaining hidden from others, and preserve forever, in greater secrecy, the necessary judgment in Saxon land. They also used certain alphabets among themselves for a time, but these at last fell entirely out of use. Yet the office of this sort of inquiry remains down to the present time, and its ministers are commonly called Feimeri.

Although these characters do not appear sufficiently beautiful and graceful, nevertheless it pleased me to join them to my newly devised alphabets set out above, for the honor and reverence of antiquity, since there is no one, even moderately industrious, who could not devise more beautiful ones for himself according to his own judgment. Yet continued practice in writing will be able, by easy compensation, to amend this misshapen crookedness, and, when often brought into habit, to make it pleasing. For no one, however learned or educated he may be, and even if he is a good writer in any language, will have turned practice into art unless through long habit. Another follows.

If anyone wishes to fashion a new alphabet for himself according to his own judgment, he should know that two things in particular must be observed, though I do not always find them preserved in the preceding examples. The first is that he form characters easy to handle; the simpler they are, the better they will be. The second is that he so arrange the individual letters that, when joined into words, they present a beautiful and graceful appearance. I add a third, not previously promised: if you wish the writing to remain secret, make some letters of the alphabet double, after the manner of the Hebrews.

This Hichus the Frank came with Marcomer from the borders of Scythia to the mouths of the Rhine in Germany, and is said to have invented these characters as a supplement to the German language; they also seem in many respects to agree sufficiently with it. Pharamund too, king of the Franks in Germany, the forty-third after Marcomer, who had introduced the people, and who died in the year 426 from the Lord's Nativity, when he had sent his scouts through Gaul for the sake of invasion, ordered them to use such characters in writing.

From Latin letters also many alphabets are made, when they are either reduced to number according to the example that we handed down in the preceding fifth book, or, in another way, after the imitation of the Northmen, when together with Greek letters they provide a method, even with our own letters, for communicating whatever we wish with the greatest security. Let us therefore set the letters themselves, altered in an unusual way, according to the number and order of our ordinary alphabet, and we shall have a secret and mystical method of writing, sufficiently beautiful and graceful once confirmed by practice. This method of numbering, however, differs from the one that we placed in the fifth book, and is more suitable to our present purpose.

I have also found another alphabet, newly devised by a certain alchemist, which he was accustomed to use for hiding his secrets, making great estimate of his art, though it contains little in itself besides words. For alchemy is loved by many, and is chaste.

Alchemy has many domestic attendants, who guard their mistress with perpetual vigilance and put themselves forward under her name, so that they may preserve her forever untouched from commerce with so many importunate lovers. Vanity, fraud, deceitfulness, deception, sophistry, greed, falsehood, lying confidence, foolishness, want, poverty, despair, flight, outlawry, and beggary are the handmaids of alchemy; pretending that their mistress is beloved, they guard her inviolate, and gladly prostitute themselves to her moneyed, avaricious, greedy, and foolish seekers.

DE

--- Page 604 ---

ON THE SIGNS AND THE WONDROUS BUT EXCESSIVELY LABORIOUS METHOD OF WRITING OF M. T. CICERO, AND AFTER HIM OF S. CYPRIAN, BISHOP AND MARTYR.

Marcus Tullius Cicero, the eloquent orator of the Romans, wrote a book of signs of no small extent. S. Cyprian, bishop of Carthage and martyr, enlarged it with many signs and words, adding vocabulary necessary for Christian uses, so that the work itself became useful not only to pagans, but much more also to the faithful.

The manuscript is rare, found by me only once, and bought at a cheap price. For when, in the year of the Lord's nativity 1496, I was examining many libraries out of love for books, I found the aforesaid manuscript in a certain monastery of our order, neglected because of its extreme age, cast away under dust and contempt. I asked the abbot, a doctor of law, how much he valued it. He answered that he would prefer to it the recently printed little works of S. Anselm. I went to the booksellers, since the matter took place in a metropolitan city, and bought the requested little works of Anselm for one sixth of a florin; to the abbot

P 4
and mo-

--- Page 605 ---
600

and to the rejoicing monks I handed them over, and I rescued the manuscript, already brought almost to destruction. For, out of love for the parchment, they had decided that it should be scraped.

Almost two years after this, I rode to Strasbourg on business of my order, and, being admitted by Ioannes Keisersberg, the distinguished preacher of the place, into the library of the greater church, I found a psalter written entirely in the same signs of Tullius and Cyprian, and most handsomely written with golden capitals. But an inscription had been placed on the outside by someone ignorant of the mystery: Psalterium in Armenica lingua. I brought in a learned man, showed the falsity, and advised that it should be rewritten thus: Psalterium notis Ciceronianis descriptum. Whether he did so or not I am uncertain, since I did not afterwards return to that library. So that you may see, reader, the form of Cicero's notation, we have decided to append a few signs.

604

HOW
WE MAY SAFELY WRITE
ANY SECRETS BY NUMBERS
AND BY MANY OTHER VARIED METHODS.

These things are childish, I admit, since each person, according to his own capacity and judgment, can devise many alphabets more elegant than those we have recorded in this volume. For that reason we knowingly omit many things which, for a similar reason, we judge to be childish, common, and of little utility. I fear that someday I shall be ashamed to have applied my mind to these trifles while more useful matters were left aside. But I beg my readers to pardon me, since my own will indeed drew me back from these childish studies, not to say at least partly vain ones; yet the insistence of certain friends compelled me to write them, and I could neither refuse nor ought to have refused them without injury to the bond of friendship contracted.

Nevertheless, moved by their request, I also had to attach the following chapter to what has been written before, and to place numbers in place of letters. For the ancients devised various and manifold methods of writing by means of numerical figures, by which, through subtle invention, they safely transmitted their secrets to one another. Persuaded therefore by the prayers of friends, we have placed at the end of this sixth book a few methods out of many, by which a path to knowledge of the others may be open to those who do not know them. For if anyone takes pleasure in knowing these things, he will easily be able, once these are known, to ascend to higher things, and to vary the invented methods at his own discretion by manifold alternation.

As the beginning of this matter, we have placed a table under the orchema, which, when its reading is varied in the form of a cross, will contain in itself ninety-six alphabets.
